## 11. Diaphragm

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 11.4 Axial Muscles of the Abdominal Wall and Thorax; 22.3 The Process of Breathing; 13.4; 23.3 · id `diaphragm` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The dome-shaped sheet of muscle between the chest and the abdomen: the main muscle of breathing. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2974297; 22.3 ¶fs-id1882180]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The diaphragm is a dome of muscle under your lungs. It pulls air in when it tightens. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2974297; 22.3 ¶fs-id1882180]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its contraction flattens the dome, enlarging the thoracic cavity and lowering the pressure in the lungs. <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id1882180]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It separates the thoracic and abdominal cavities. Its upper surface is the floor of the chest. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2974297]</sub>
- Its muscle arises from the xiphoid process, the lower six ribs and cartilages, and the lumbar vertebrae. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2304478]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits across the bottom of your rib cage, under your heart and lungs. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2974297; 11.4 ¶fs-id2304478]</sub>

*Deeper.* The pericardial sac and the parietal pleura fuse onto its central tendon. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2304478]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Central tendon.** The tendon in the middle of the dome, into which all the muscle fibres insert. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2304478; 11.4 ¶tbl-ch11_07]</sub>
- **Caval opening.** Lets the inferior vena cava pass from the abdomen to the thorax. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2143601]</sub>
- **Oesophageal hiatus.** Lets the oesophagus and its nerves pass into the abdomen. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2143601; 23.3 ¶fs-id1984077]</sub>
- **Aortic hiatus.** At the back: the aorta, thoracic duct and azygos vein pass through it. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2143601]</sub>

*Simpler.* It has a tendon in the middle and three openings for big tubes to pass through. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2304478; 11.4 ¶fs-id2143601]</sub>

*Deeper.* The three openings lie at about the levels of T8 (caval), T10 (oesophageal) and T12 (aortic). † <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2143601; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The vertebral levels of the diaphragm’s openings.]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Contracting, it moves down towards the abdomen and makes the thoracic cavity larger. <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id1882180]</sub>
- The lungs follow, because pleural fluid holds them to the chest wall. Their pressure falls and air flows in. <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id1882180; 22.3 ¶fs-id1545698]</sub>
- Quiet expiration is passive: the diaphragm relaxes and the elastic lungs recoil. <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id2453488]</sub>

*Simpler.* Tighten it and air comes in. Relax it and air goes out by itself. <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id1882180; 22.3 ¶fs-id2453488]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *muscle* **External intercostals.** Work with the diaphragm in quiet inspiration, lifting the ribs up and out. <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id1882180; 22.3 ¶fs-id2327717]</sub>
- *muscle* **Abdominal muscles.** With the diaphragm, they raise abdominal pressure in defecation, urination and childbirth. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2072110]</sub>
- *nerve* **Phrenic nerve.** The phrenic nerve comes from the cervical plexus in the neck. It reaches the diaphragm at the base of the thorax. <sub>[13.4 ¶fs-id1472046]</sub>
- *vessel* **Inferior vena cava and aorta.** Pass through the caval opening and the aortic hiatus. <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2143601]</sub>
- *organ* **Oesophagus.** Enters the abdomen through the oesophageal hiatus. <sub>[23.3 ¶fs-id1984077; 11.4 ¶fs-id2143601]</sub>

*Simpler.* The heart and lungs sit on it and the liver and stomach lie under it. A nerve from the neck works it. † <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2974297; 13.4 ¶fs-id1472046; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the liver and stomach lie directly under the diaphragm.]</sub>

### 6. Development

- The phrenic nerve comes from the neck (cervical plexus), a sign that the diaphragm’s muscle starts in the neck region. † <sub>[13.4 ¶fs-id1472046; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The development of the diaphragm and why its nerve comes from the neck.]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- A deep breath is mainly diaphragmatic; a shallow breath relies more on the intercostals (costal breathing). <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id2276812]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Damage to the pons or medulla, after a stroke or from drugs such as morphine.  
   **Damaged:** The respiratory centres that drive the diaphragm.  
   **Patient can’t:** Breathe regularly in sleep: breathing stops for short periods (central sleep apnoea).  
   **Why:** Without signals from the centres, the diaphragm and intercostals do not contract. <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id2582598; 22.3 ¶fs-id2096099]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Part of the stomach slides up through the oesophageal hiatus (a hiatus hernia).  
   **Damaged:** The oesophageal hiatus, which has widened.  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep stomach acid out of the oesophagus: heartburn is worse lying down.  
   **Why:** The hiatus no longer holds the junction of the oesophagus and stomach below the diaphragm. † <sub>[23.3 ¶fs-id1984077; 11.4 ¶fs-id2143601; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Hiatus hernia and its symptoms.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how contraction of the diaphragm draws air into the lungs.** <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id1882180; 22.3 ¶fs-id1545698]</sub>

   - The diaphragm moves down and the thoracic cavity enlarges.
   - Pleural fluid makes the lungs follow the chest wall.
   - Lung volume rises, so pressure in the alveoli falls below atmospheric.
   - Air flows down this pressure gradient into the lungs.

2. *Compare.* **Compare quiet and forced breathing.** <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id2327717; 22.3 ¶fs-id2146002; 22.3 ¶fs-id2453488]</sub>

   - Quiet breathing: the diaphragm and external intercostals contract; expiration is passive.
   - Forced breathing: inspiration and expiration both use muscles.
   - Neck muscles such as the scalenes help forced inspiration.
   - Abdominal muscles and internal intercostals drive forced expiration.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what happens to breathing if the phrenic nerve is cut on one side.** † <sub>[13.4 ¶fs-id1472046; 22.3 ¶fs-id1882180; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The effects of cutting one phrenic nerve.]</sub>

   - The phrenic nerve is the diaphragm’s motor nerve.
   - That half of the diaphragm cannot contract.
   - Inspiration on that side weakens.
   - The intercostals and accessory muscles must do more of the work.

### Check yourself

1. What passes through the aortic hiatus?  
   A. Inferior vena cava · B. Oesophagus · **C. Aorta, thoracic duct and azygos vein** · D. Phrenic nerve  
   *The aorta, thoracic duct and azygos vein pass through the aortic hiatus at the back.* <sub>[11.4 ¶fs-id2143601]</sub>

2. Which nerve supplies the diaphragm?  
   A. Vagus nerve · **B. Phrenic nerve** · C. Intercostal nerve · D. Accessory nerve  
   *The phrenic nerve, from the cervical plexus, connects to the diaphragm.* <sub>[13.4 ¶fs-id1472046]</sub>

3. What happens when the diaphragm contracts?  
   **A. It moves inferiorly and the thoracic cavity enlarges** · B. It moves superiorly and air is forced out · C. The lungs actively expand themselves · D. Intra-alveolar pressure rises above atmospheric  
   *It moves down towards the abdomen, enlarging the thoracic cavity.* <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id1882180]</sub>

## 12. Ethmoid bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 7.5; 14.1 · id `ethmoid` · bone `ethmoid_bone` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A single midline bone between the orbits that forms the roof and upper walls of the nasal cavity (ethmoid bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1971405]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The ethmoid bone sits behind the top of your nose, between your eyes. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1971405; 7.2 ¶fs-id1897403]</sub>

*Deeper.* The nerve fibres for smell pass through its cribriform plate into the brain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415; 14.1 ¶fs-id2351449]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It lies in the midline, between the orbit and the upper nasal cavity on each side. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1971405; 7.2 ¶fs-id1897403]</sub>
- Inside the skull, it forms a small midline area of the floor of the anterior cranial fossa. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415]</sub>
- It forms the upper nasal septum and part of the medial wall of each orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1971405]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is deep behind the bridge of your nose, between your eye sockets. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1971405; 7.2 ¶fs-id1897403]</sub>

*Deeper.* Though counted with the brain case, it also helps form the nasal septum, nasal walls and orbits. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1861377]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Crista galli.** A small upward projection in the midline (“rooster’s comb”). It anchors one of the coverings of the brain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415]</sub>
- **Cribriform plates.** Flat plates either side of it (“sieve”), pierced by small olfactory foramina. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415]</sub>
- **Perpendicular plate.** Forms the upper part of the nasal septum. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2228601; 7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>
- **Superior and middle nasal conchae.** Thin curved plates projecting from each side wall of the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2228601; 7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>
- **Ethmoid air cells.** Many small air spaces with very thin walls, between the nasal cavity and the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1897403; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>
- **Orbital surface.** Forms much of the medial wall of each orbit, with the lacrimal bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1897403; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>

*Simpler.* It has a sieve-like roof, a central plate in the nose and two scroll-like plates on each side. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415; 7.2 ¶fs-id2228601; 7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

*Deeper.* The superior concha is the smallest; it is hidden above and behind the middle concha. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Its cribriform plates are pierced by olfactory foramina, letting the fibres for smell reach the brain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415; 14.1 ¶fs-id2351449]</sub>
- Its curved conchae swirl incoming air, helping to warm and moisten it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>
- Mucus on the conchae traps dust, pollen, bacteria and viruses. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>
- Its air cells are part of the paranasal sinuses, which lighten the skull. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1897403; 7.2 ¶fs-id1977054]</sub>

*Simpler.* It lets the nerves for smell through, and helps warm and clean the air you breathe. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415; 7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *nerve* **Olfactory nerve.** Its fibres pass from the nasal lining through the cribriform plate to the olfactory bulb. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2351449; 7.2 ¶fs-id1977415]</sub>
- *brain* **Frontal lobe.** The olfactory bulb lies on its underside, just above the cribriform plate. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2351449]</sub>
- *bone* **Vomer.** With the perpendicular plate and the septal cartilage, forms the nasal septum. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>
- *bone* **Lacrimal bone.** Together they form much of the medial wall of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- *bone* **Frontal bone.** Forms most of the floor of the anterior cranial fossa, around the ethmoid’s small midline part. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2045358]</sub>
- *covering* **Covering of the brain.** One of the layers covering the brain is anchored to the crista galli. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415]</sub>

*Simpler.* The nerves for smell pass through it to the brain, and it joins the bones of the nose and orbit. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2351449; 7.2 ¶fs-id2125356; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Like the bones of the skull base, it forms from a cartilage model (endochondral ossification). <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id2251103]</sub>
- The paranasal sinuses, including its air cells, enlarge during childhood. <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1241553]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Blunt force to the face, as in a car accident.  
   **Damaged:** The olfactory nerve fibres are sheared where they pass through the cribriform plate.  
   **Patient can’t:** Smell (anosmia); food may taste bland.  
   **Why:** The frontal lobe moves relative to the ethmoid bone, tearing the fine fibres. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2817840; 14.1 ¶fs-id2441552]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A fracture through the cribriform plate.  
   **Damaged:** The coverings of the brain over the plate tear.  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep the nose dry: the clear fluid around the brain (cerebrospinal fluid) leaks from the nose.  
   **Why:** The thin plate is all that separates the nasal cavity from the cranial cavity. † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415; † Leakage of cerebrospinal fluid from the nose is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the plate’s position is.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how a blow to the face can cause loss of smell.** <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2351449; 14.1 ¶fs-id2817840]</sub>

   - Olfactory nerve fibres pass through the cribriform plate of the ethmoid bone.
   - They reach the olfactory bulb on the underside of the frontal lobe.
   - In a blow, the frontal lobe moves relative to the ethmoid bone.
   - The fibres are sheared, causing anosmia.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the perpendicular plate of the ethmoid with the vomer.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

   - Both form part of the bony nasal septum.
   - The perpendicular plate forms its upper part.
   - The triangular vomer forms its lower and back parts.
   - Septal cartilage fills the gap between them at the front.

3. *Predict.* **Predict which nasal conchae you would see looking into the front of a dry skull, and explain why.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

   - The inferior and middle conchae.
   - The inferior concha is the largest.
   - The middle concha, part of the ethmoid, lies just above it.
   - The small superior concha is hidden above and behind the middle one.

### Check yourself

1. What passes through the cribriform plate?  
   A. The optic nerve · **B. Fibres of the olfactory nerve** · C. The internal carotid artery · D. The facial nerve  
   *Olfactory axons pass through the olfactory foramina of the cribriform plate.* <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2351449; 7.2 ¶fs-id1977415]</sub>

2. Which nasal conchae are part of the ethmoid bone?  
   **A. Superior and middle** · B. Inferior only · C. Middle and inferior · D. All three  
   *The superior and middle conchae belong to the ethmoid; the inferior concha is a separate bone.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

3. What does the crista galli anchor?  
   A. The nasal septum · B. The olfactory bulb · C. The eyeball · **D. One of the coverings of the brain**  
   *It is an anterior attachment point for one of the covering layers of the brain.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977415]</sub>

---

## 13. Frontal bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 7.5; 9.2; 14.1 · id `frontal` · bone `frontal_bone` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The single bone of the forehead, which also forms the roof of each orbit (frontal bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The frontal bone is your forehead bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>

*Deeper.* Inside the skull, it forms most of the floor of the anterior cranial fossa. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984; 7.2 ¶fs-id2045358]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It forms the forehead, at the front of the brain case. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984; 7.2 ¶fs-id1351960]</sub>
- Behind, it meets the two parietal bones at the coronal suture. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- Below, it forms the upper margin and the roof of each orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>

*Simpler.* It forms your forehead and the top of your eye sockets. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>

*Deeper.* It is one of the eight brain case bones, and one of four that meet at the pterion. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1351960; 7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Glabella.** A slight hollow between the eyebrows, at the front midline. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>
- **Supraorbital margin.** The upper rim of each orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>
- **Supraorbital foramen.** An opening near the middle of each margin, for a sensory nerve to the forehead. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>
- **Brow ridges.** Rounded thickenings just above each margin, behind the eyebrows. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>
- **Orbital part.** A flat region that forms the roof of the orbits and the floor of the anterior cranial fossa. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>
- **Frontal sinus.** An air-filled space just above the eyebrows: the most anterior paranasal sinus. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

*Simpler.* It has a curved front, rims over the eyes and a flat part extending back inside the skull. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>

*Deeper.* The frontal sinus may be one space, or divided at the midline into left and right. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Its curved front encloses the frontal lobes of the brain, which lie in the anterior cranial fossa. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2045358; 7.2 ¶fs-id1256378]</sub>
- The thickened rims of the orbits help protect the eyes. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1883481; 7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>
- Its sinus lightens the skull and adds resonance to the voice. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977054; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

*Simpler.* It shields the front of the brain and the tops of the eyes. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2045358; 7.2 ¶fs-id1883481]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *suture* **Coronal suture.** Joins it to the right and left parietal bones, across the top of the skull. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- *landmark* **Pterion.** Where the frontal, parietal, temporal and sphenoid bones meet: the weakest part of the skull. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- *brain* **Frontal lobes.** Lie in the anterior cranial fossa, which the frontal bone mostly floors. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2045358]</sub>
- *bone* **Ethmoid bone.** Its crista galli and cribriform plates lie in the midline of the same floor. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2045358]</sub>
- *nerve* **Sensory nerve to the forehead.** Passes through the supraorbital foramen. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>
- *space* **Orbit.** The frontal bone forms its roof. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>

*Simpler.* It joins the parietal bones behind and roofs the eye sockets below. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Like the other bones of the top and sides of the brain case, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification). <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1547037]</sub>
- At birth, it is in two halves, which fuse in early childhood. <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1241553]</sub>
- The anterior fontanelle, the soft spot at the front of a baby’s head, lies where it meets the parietal bones. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- The brow ridges vary in size and are generally larger in males. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>
- The frontal sinus may be a single space or two. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A cold or sinus congestion.  
   **Damaged:** The lining of the frontal sinus swells and blocks its narrow passage to the nasal cavity.  
   **Patient can’t:** Be free of pressure and pain above the eyebrows.  
   **Why:** The blocked sinus fills with fluid, and the pressure causes pain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977054; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A strong blow to the forehead.  
   **Damaged:** The bone breaks; in a depressed fracture, it is pushed inwards.  
   **Patient can’t:** Escape the risk of bleeding inside the skull, which can injure the brain.  
   **Why:** The frontal lobes lie directly behind the bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1972896; 7.2 ¶fs-id2045358]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the frontal bone takes part in both the orbit and the cranial cavity.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927; 7.2 ¶fs-id2045358]</sub>

   - Its front forms the forehead and the supraorbital margins.
   - Inside the skull it extends backwards as a flat region.
   - This region forms the roof of the orbits below.
   - And it forms most of the floor of the anterior cranial fossa above.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the frontal bone with the parietal bones.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984; 7.2 ¶fs-id1975652; 7.2 ¶fs-id1248497; 7.5 ¶fs-id1547037]</sub>

   - The frontal bone is single and forms the forehead.
   - The parietal bones are paired and form most of the upper sides of the skull.
   - They meet at the coronal suture.
   - All of them form directly from membrane.

3. *Predict.* **Predict where a patient with a blocked frontal sinus feels pain, and explain why.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882870; 7.2 ¶fs-id1977054]</sub>

   - Just above the eyebrows, where the frontal sinus lies.
   - Swollen lining blocks the narrow passage to the nasal cavity.
   - The sinus fills with fluid.
   - The rising pressure causes pain.

### Check yourself

1. Which suture joins the frontal bone to the parietal bones?  
   A. Sagittal · **B. Coronal** · C. Lambdoid · D. Squamous  
   *The coronal suture runs across the skull between them.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

2. What is the glabella?  
   **A. The hollow between the eyebrows** · B. The opening for the nerve to the forehead · C. The brow ridge · D. The roof of the orbit  
   *The glabella is the slight depression at the front midline, between the eyebrows.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1283984]</sub>

3. Which part of the brain lies behind the frontal bone, in the anterior cranial fossa?  
   A. Cerebellum · B. Temporal lobes · C. Brainstem · **D. Frontal lobes**  
   *The anterior cranial fossa contains the frontal lobes.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2045358]</sub>

## 14. Hamate

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (and glossary); 8.5; 9.4 · id `hamate` · bone `hamate_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The hooked wrist bone on the little-finger side, fourth in the second row of carpal bones (hamate). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: hamate]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The hamate is the wrist bone below your ring and little fingers. <sub>[glossary: hamate; 8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

*Deeper.* A hook projects from its front, and it anchors the inner end of the flexor retinaculum. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; 8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It is the fourth bone of the distal row, counting from the thumb side. <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>
- Above it are the lunate and triquetrum. The capitate is on its thumb side. <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>
- Below it are the fourth and fifth metacarpals. <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits on the little-finger side of the wrist, below the ring and little fingers. <sub>[glossary: hamate; 8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

*Deeper.* The metacarpals it carries can move a little forwards and backwards, unlike the second and third. <sub>[glossary: hamate; 8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Name.** “Hamate” means hooked bone. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- **Hook of the hamate.** A prominent bony extension on its front. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: hook of the hamate bone]</sub>
- **Surfaces for the fourth and fifth metacarpals.** Its lower surface, at the carpometacarpal joints. <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>
- **Upper surfaces.** It meets the lunate and triquetrum above. <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>
- **Side surface.** It meets the capitate on the thumb side. <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>

*Simpler.* The hamate is a small bone with a hook on its front. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

*Deeper.* On the little-finger side, the flexor retinaculum attaches to it and to the pisiform. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- With the pisiform, it anchors the inner end of the flexor retinaculum. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>
- It carries the fourth and fifth metacarpals, which move a little during a power grip. <sub>[glossary: hamate; 8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>
- The forward movement of the fifth metacarpal strengthens the grip of the inner hand. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

*Simpler.* It anchors the carpal tunnel’s roof and supports the palm bones of the ring and little fingers. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218; glossary: hamate]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joint* **Midcarpal joint.** Its upper surfaces meet the lunate and triquetrum. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id932152; glossary: hamate]</sub>
- *bones* **Fourth and fifth metacarpals.** Meet its lower surface. <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>
- *bone* **Capitate.** Its neighbour on the thumb side. <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>
- *ligament* **Flexor retinaculum.** Attached on the little-finger side to the hamate and pisiform. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>
- *nerve* **Ulnar nerve.** Passes beside the hook of the hamate into the hand. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

*Simpler.* It joins the first row above, the capitate beside it and the palm bones of the ring and little fingers. <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification), like all limb bones except the clavicle. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Its single ossification centre appears during the first year, just after the capitate. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives the first year, after the capitate (supporting only).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Overuse of the tendons or a wrist injury makes the contents of the carpal tunnel swell.  
   **Damaged:** The nerve in the tunnel, whose walls and floor the carpal bones form.  
   **Patient can’t:** Use the hand without pain, numbness or weakness (carpal tunnel syndrome).  
   **Why:** The bony walls and floor and the tough roof cannot stretch, so swelling presses on the nerve. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A golf club, bat or racquet handle strikes the base of the palm.  
   **Damaged:** The hook of the hamate breaks.  
   **Patient can’t:** Grip firmly without pain at the base of the palm.  
   **Why:** The handle presses directly on the hook, which projects forwards. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the fourth and fifth metacarpals strengthen a power grip, and the hamate’s part.** <sub>[glossary: hamate; 8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

   - The hamate carries the fourth and fifth metacarpals.
   - These metacarpals can move a little forwards and backwards.
   - The fifth moves most.
   - Moving forwards in a grip, they press the inner hand firmly against the object.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the two ends of the flexor retinaculum.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

   - On the thumb side it attaches to the trapezium and scaphoid.
   - On the little-finger side it attaches to the hamate and pisiform.
   - It spans the top of the U-shaped carpal bones.
   - So it forms the roof of the carpal tunnel.

3. *Predict.* **A golfer strikes the ground and has pain at the base of the palm. Predict the fracture and why.** † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

   - A fracture of the hook of the hamate.
   - The club handle lies across the base of the palm.
   - The hook projects forwards, so the handle presses on it.
   - Gripping is painful.

### Check yourself

1. What projects from the front of the hamate?  
   A. A styloid process · **B. A hook** · C. A tuberosity · D. A head  
   *The hook of the hamate projects from its front.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

2. Which metacarpals does the hamate meet?  
   A. First and second · B. Second and third · **C. Fourth and fifth** · D. Third only  
   *It meets the fourth and fifth metacarpals.* <sub>[glossary: hamate]</sub>

3. Which carpal bones anchor the inner (medial) end of the flexor retinaculum?  
   A. Trapezium and scaphoid · B. Lunate and triquetrum · C. Capitate and trapezoid · **D. Hamate and pisiform**  
   *Medially, the flexor retinaculum attaches to the hamate and pisiform.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

---

## 15. Hamstrings

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs; 11.1; 13.4 · id `hamstrings` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The three long muscles at the back of the thigh that bend the knee and extend the hip. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638; glossary: hamstring group]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The hamstrings are the muscles at the back of your thigh. They bend your knee. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>

*Deeper.* They are the biceps femoris, semitendinosus and semimembranosus, in the posterior compartment of the thigh. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- The hamstrings fill the posterior compartment of the thigh. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>
- All three arise from the ischial tuberosity; the biceps femoris also from the linea aspera and distal femur. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>

*Simpler.* They run from the bone you sit on to just below the back of the knee. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03; 11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>

*Deeper.* Their tendons form the borders of the popliteal fossa, the diamond-shaped space behind the knee. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Biceps femoris.** The lateral hamstring. Besides flexing the knee, it turns the thigh outwards. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>
- **Semitendinosus.** A medial hamstring with a long tendon. It turns the thigh inwards. † <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The long tendon of the semitendinosus.]</sub>
- **Semimembranosus.** A medial hamstring, deep to the semitendinosus. It turns the thigh inwards. † <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the semimembranosus lies deep to the semitendinosus.]</sub>
- **Popliteal fossa.** The diamond-shaped space behind the knee, bordered by their tendons. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>

*Simpler.* One hamstring is on the outer side; two are on the inner side. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>

*Deeper.* The biceps femoris inserts on the head of the fibula; the other two on the medial side of the tibia. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The insertions of the hamstrings.]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Crossing the back of the knee, they flex the leg, as when kneeling. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638; 11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>
- Crossing the back of the hip, they also extend the thigh: they move it down and back. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638; 11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>
- They oppose the quadriceps, which extend the knee. <sub>[11.1 ¶tbl-ch11_01]</sub>

*Simpler.* They bend the knee and swing the thigh backwards. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638; 11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Ischial tuberosity.** Their common origin, on the hip bone. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>
- *muscle* **Quadriceps femoris.** Their antagonists at the knee. <sub>[11.1 ¶tbl-ch11_01]</sub>
- *nerve* **Sciatic nerve.** Runs down the back of the thigh. It is the tibial and fibular nerves combined. † <sub>[13.4 ¶fs-id1472046; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the sciatic nerve supplies the hamstrings and runs among them.]</sub>
- *muscle* **Gastrocnemius.** Arises from the femoral condyles, between the hamstring tendons behind the knee. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_05]</sub>
- *space* **Popliteal fossa.** Bounded above by the hamstring tendons. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>

*Simpler.* They sit behind the thigh, opposite the quadriceps, with the sciatic nerve close by. <sub>[11.1 ¶tbl-ch11_01; 13.4 ¶fs-id1472046]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Like all limb muscles, they develop from general mesoderm, and each fibre forms from many fused myoblasts. <sub>[10.9 ¶fs-id1959103]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- Sprinters’ muscles have a high share of fast glycolytic fibres for powerful bursts. † <sub>[10.6 ¶fs-id2264056; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Fibre types of the hamstrings specifically.]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A sudden sprint, with the hip flexed and the knee straightening.  
   **Damaged:** The hamstrings, which tear (a hamstring strain).  
   **Patient can’t:** Run or bend the knee against resistance without pain.  
   **Why:** They are stretched across two joints while contracting hard to slow the leg. † <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Hamstring strains.]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Compression of the sciatic nerve or the spinal nerves that form it.  
   **Damaged:** The sciatic nerve (sciatica).  
   **Patient can’t:** Move the hip and leg without pain.  
   **Why:** Compression or irritation of the nerve, or of the spinal nerves forming it, causes sciatica. <sub>[13.4 ¶fs-id1472046]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the hamstrings act on both the hip and the knee.** <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03; 11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>

   - They arise from the ischial tuberosity, behind the hip joint.
   - They cross the back of the knee to the leg.
   - So they extend the thigh at the hip.
   - And they flex the leg at the knee.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the biceps femoris with the semitendinosus and semimembranosus.** <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>

   - All three flex the knee and extend the thigh.
   - The biceps femoris lies laterally and turns the thigh outwards.
   - The other two lie medially and turn it inwards.
   - Only the biceps femoris also arises from the femur.

3. *Predict.* **Predict which movement is hard after a hamstring strain.** † <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638; 11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Hamstring strains.]</sub>

   - Bending the knee against resistance.
   - Swinging the thigh back while running.
   - Both stretch or load the torn muscle.
   - So running and climbing hurt most.

### Check yourself

1. Where do all three hamstrings arise?  
   **A. Ischial tuberosity** · B. Anterior inferior iliac spine · C. Greater trochanter · D. Pubic symphysis  
   *All three arise from the ischial tuberosity.* <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>

2. What space do the hamstring tendons help to form?  
   A. Femoral triangle · **B. Popliteal fossa** · C. Inguinal canal · D. Tarsal tunnel  
   *Their tendons form the popliteal fossa behind the knee.* <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>

3. Which hamstring turns the thigh outwards?  
   A. Semitendinosus · B. Semimembranosus · C. Gracilis · **D. Biceps femoris**  
   *The biceps femoris twists the thigh outwards; the other two twist it inwards.* <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>

---

## 16. Phalanges of the hand

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (and glossary); 8.5; 9.4; 9.5; 20.5 · id `hand-phalanges` · bone `hand_proximal_phalanx_3_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The 14 bones of the fingers and thumb (phalanges). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490; glossary: phalanx bone of the hand]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The phalanges are the bones of your fingers and thumb. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>

*Deeper.* The name comes from the Greek “phalanx”, a rectangular block of soldiers. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- The thumb (digit 1) has two: a proximal and a distal phalanx. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>
- Each finger, digits 2 to 5, has three: proximal, middle and distal. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>
- The proximal phalanges meet the heads of the metacarpals at the knuckles. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

*Simpler.* There are two in your thumb and three in each finger. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>

*Deeper.* The anatomical name for the thumb is the pollex. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490; glossary: pollex]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Proximal phalanges.** The nearest to the palm; each meets a metacarpal head at a knuckle joint. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490; 8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>
- **Middle phalanges.** Only in the fingers, between the proximal and distal phalanges. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>
- **Distal phalanges.** The tips of the fingers and thumb. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>
- **Interphalangeal joints.** The joints between neighbouring phalanges. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490; glossary: interphalangeal joint]</sub>
- **Base, shaft and head.** Each phalanx has a base nearest the palm, a shaft and a head. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

*Simpler.* Each finger has a near, a middle and an end bone; the thumb has no middle one. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>

*Deeper.* So the thumb has one interphalangeal joint, and each finger has two. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Interphalangeal joints are hinge joints: they only bend and straighten. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id2523908]</sub>
- Knuckle (metacarpophalangeal) joints are condyloid, so the fingers can bend and also spread apart. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1434553]</sub>
- Spreading the fingers apart is abduction; bringing them together is adduction. <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1751608]</sub>

*Simpler.* Knuckle joints bend and spread; the joints along the fingers only bend. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1434553; 9.4 ¶fs-id2523908]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joints* **Metacarpophalangeal (knuckle) joints.** Condyloid joints between the metacarpal heads and the proximal phalanges. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1434553; glossary: metacarpophalangeal joint]</sub>
- *joints* **Interphalangeal joints.** Hinge joints between neighbouring phalanges. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id2523908; glossary: interphalangeal joint]</sub>
- *bones* **Metacarpals.** Meet the proximal phalanges at the knuckles. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>
- *arteries* **Digital arteries.** Branch from the palmar arches to supply the fingers. <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id1595346]</sub>
- *veins* **Digital veins.** Drain the fingers into the palmar venous arches. <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id3239091]</sub>

*Simpler.* They hinge on each other and join the palm bones at the knuckles. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id2523908; 8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

### 6. Development

- They form by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification). <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Small bones like these develop only one secondary ossification centre, so each has one growth plate. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1972676]</sub>
- A growth plate is cartilage, less dense than bone, so it looks dark on an X-ray. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1972676]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A child’s injured finger has an X-ray.  
   **Damaged:** There may be no injury: each phalanx has a normal growth plate.  
   **Patient can’t:** Rely on one dark line alone to diagnose a fracture.  
   **Why:** Cartilage is less dense than bone, so a normal plate looks dark and can be mistaken for a break. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1972676]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A ball strikes the tip of a straight finger.  
   **Damaged:** The tendon that straightens the fingertip tears from the distal phalanx, sometimes with a fragment of bone (mallet finger).  
   **Patient can’t:** Straighten the tip of the finger.  
   **Why:** Without its tendon, nothing pulls the distal phalanx straight. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the thumb has one interphalangeal joint, but each finger has two.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>

   - The thumb has two phalanges: proximal and distal.
   - Each finger has three: proximal, middle and distal.
   - An interphalangeal joint lies between each pair of neighbouring phalanges.
   - So the thumb has one, and each finger has two.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the knuckle (metacarpophalangeal) joints with the interphalangeal joints.** <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1434553; 9.4 ¶fs-id2523908]</sub>

   - Knuckle joints are condyloid: biaxial.
   - They allow bending and straightening, and spreading and closing the fingers.
   - Interphalangeal joints are hinge joints: uniaxial.
   - They allow only bending and straightening.

3. *Predict.* **A cricket ball strikes the tip of a straight finger, and the tip now droops. Predict the injury and explain it.** † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

   - Mallet finger.
   - The tendon that straightens the fingertip has torn from the distal phalanx.
   - A small piece of bone may come away with it.
   - Nothing now pulls the fingertip straight.

### Check yourself

1. How many phalanges are in each hand?  
   A. 5 · B. 10 · **C. 14** · D. 19  
   *The thumb has two and each finger three: 14 in all.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>

2. What type of joint is an interphalangeal joint?  
   A. Condyloid · **B. Hinge** · C. Saddle · D. Plane  
   *Interphalangeal joints are hinge joints.* <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id2523908]</sub>

3. Which digit has no middle phalanx?  
   A. Index finger · B. Middle finger · C. Little finger · **D. Thumb**  
   *The thumb has only proximal and distal phalanges.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>

---

## 17. Heart

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 19.1 Heart Anatomy; 19.5 Development of the Heart · id `heart` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The four-chambered muscular pump in the middle of the chest that drives blood round the lungs and the body. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1892589; 19.1 ¶fs-id1478856; 19.1 ¶fs-id2767147]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The heart is a pump about the size of your fist. It pushes blood all round your body. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2694723; 19.1 ¶fs-id1478856]</sub>

*Deeper.* The right side of the heart feeds the pulmonary circuit; the left side feeds the systemic circuit. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2767147; 19.1 ¶fs-id1236860]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It lies in the mediastinum, between the lungs, inside the pericardial sac. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1892589]</sub>
- Its base, where the great vessels attach, is at the level of the third costal cartilage. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1892589]</sub>
- Its apex lies just left of the sternum, between the fourth and fifth ribs near their cartilages. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1892589]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits behind your breastbone, pointing down and to the left. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1892589]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its right side is turned forwards and its left side backwards. The left lung has a notch for it. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1892589; 22.2 ¶fs-id2627237]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Atria.** The two upper chambers. They receive blood and push it into the ventricles. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1478856]</sub>
- **Ventricles.** The two lower chambers, the main pumps: right to the lungs, left to the body. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1478856]</sub>
- **Valves.** Tricuspid and mitral between atria and ventricles; pulmonary and aortic at the exits. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2718087; 19.1 ¶fs-id2268819]</sub>
- **Septa.** Walls between the chambers: interatrial, interventricular and atrioventricular. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2301176; 19.1 ¶fs-id1422157; 19.1 ¶fs-id2268819]</sub>
- **Myocardium.** The thick middle layer of the wall, made of cardiac muscle that pumps the blood. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2062267; 19.1 ¶fs-id2122250]</sub>

*Simpler.* Four chambers, four one-way valves and a thick muscle wall. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1478856; 19.1 ¶fs-id2718087]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its muscle cells swirl round the chambers in figure-of-eight patterns, which helps it pump efficiently. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2062267]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- The left ventricle’s wall is much thicker than the right’s, to pump against the higher resistance of the systemic circuit. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2183171]</sub>
- Papillary muscles tighten the chordae tendineae as the ventricles contract, so the valve flaps cannot blow back. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2765308; 19.1 ¶fs-id1534517]</sub>
- The semilunar valves are pockets that fill with blood flowing back, sealing the exits. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2541385]</sub>

*Simpler.* Thick walls for pumping hard, and valves that let blood go only one way. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2183171; 19.1 ¶fs-id2718087]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *membrane* **Pericardium.** A tough fibrous sac with a serous lining that protects the heart and holds it in place. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2418328]</sub>
- *artery* **Aorta.** Leaves the left ventricle through the aortic valve, carrying blood to the body. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2663012; 19.1 ¶fs-id1236860]</sub>
- *artery* **Pulmonary trunk.** Leaves the right ventricle and divides into the right and left pulmonary arteries. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1236860]</sub>
- *artery* **Coronary arteries.** The first branches of the aorta. They supply the heart muscle itself. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1247110]</sub>
- *vein* **Pulmonary veins.** Bring oxygen-rich blood from the lungs into the left atrium. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1236860; 19.1 ¶fs-id2769216]</sub>

*Simpler.* Big vessels leave from its top, and its own arteries wrap round its surface. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1892589; 19.1 ¶fs-id1247110]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It is the first organ to work, beating from about day 21 or 22 after fertilisation. <sub>[19.5 ¶fs-id2058606]</sub>
- It forms from mesoderm as a tube, which folds into an S shape between days 23 and 28. <sub>[19.5 ¶fs-id2485730; 19.5 ¶fs-id1364320]</sub>
- In the fetus, the foramen ovale lets blood pass from the right atrium to the left. It closes at birth. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2301176]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- As many as 20–25 percent of people have a patent foramen ovale, mostly without symptoms. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2587120]</sub>
- An endurance athlete’s heart can be much larger than average, as exercise adds myofilaments. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2694723]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A coronary artery blocked by atherosclerotic plaque.  
   **Damaged:** The heart muscle the artery supplies, which dies (myocardial infarction).  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep the heart pumping normally; there is often sudden pain beneath the sternum.  
   **Why:** Without blood and oxygen, the cardiac muscle cells die. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1289761; 19.1 ¶fs-id2351428]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Fluid or blood building up quickly in the pericardial cavity.  
   **Damaged:** The heart’s filling (cardiac tamponade).  
   **Patient can’t:** Fill and empty the heart fully: less blood is pumped with each beat.  
   **Why:** The fluid presses on the heart and stops it relaxing fully between beats. <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2988974]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the left ventricle’s wall is thicker than the right’s.** <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2183171]</sub>

   - Both ventricles pump the same amount of blood per beat.
   - The left pumps into the long systemic circuit, with high resistance.
   - The right pumps into the short pulmonary circuit, with low resistance.
   - So the left must generate much more pressure.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the atrioventricular and semilunar valves.** <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2718087; 19.1 ¶fs-id2541385; 19.1 ¶fs-id2326948]</sub>

   - Atrioventricular valves lie between atria and ventricles: tricuspid on the right, mitral on the left.
   - They are held by chordae tendineae and papillary muscles.
   - Semilunar valves guard the pulmonary trunk and aorta.
   - They are pocket-like, with no chordae tendineae or papillary muscles.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what happens if chordae tendineae on the mitral valve break.** <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2266265; 19.1 ¶fs-id1932336]</sub>

   - The cusps are no longer held as the ventricle contracts.
   - A cusp is forced backwards into the atrium (prolapse).
   - Blood flows back into the left atrium (regurgitation).
   - A heart murmur is heard with a stethoscope.

### Check yourself

1. Which chamber pumps blood into the aorta?  
   A. Right atrium · B. Right ventricle · C. Left atrium · **D. Left ventricle**  
   *The left ventricle ejects blood into the aorta through the aortic semilunar valve.* <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2663012]</sub>

2. Which valve lies between the left atrium and the left ventricle?  
   **A. Mitral valve** · B. Tricuspid valve · C. Pulmonary valve · D. Aortic valve  
   *The mitral (bicuspid) valve guards the opening between the left atrium and ventricle.* <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id2326948]</sub>

3. Which vessels carry oxygen-rich blood into the heart?  
   A. Venae cavae · **B. Pulmonary veins** · C. Pulmonary arteries · D. Coronary sinus  
   *The pulmonary veins return highly oxygenated blood from the lungs to the left atrium.* <sub>[19.1 ¶fs-id1236860]</sub>

---

## 18. Hyoid bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull · id `hyoid` · bone `hyoid_bone` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A small U-shaped bone in the upper neck that touches no other bone (hyoid bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The hyoid is a small U-shaped bone in your neck, below your jaw. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

*Deeper.* It is the base of the tongue, and it moves when you swallow and speak. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It lies in the upper neck, near the level of the lower edge of the mandible. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>
- The tips of the U point backwards. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>
- It is not part of the skull: it does not touch any other bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits at the top of your neck, just under your jaw. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

*Deeper.* A ligament from the styloid process of the temporal bone helps support it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **U shape.** A small curved bone, open at the back. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>
- **Body.** The front of the U. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>
- **Tips.** The two ends of the U, pointing backwards. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>
- **Muscle attachments.** Small muscles attach to it from above and from below. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>
- **Ligament attachment.** A ligament from the styloid process of the temporal bone supports it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a small U of bone, held in place by muscles. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

*Deeper.* It is one of the very few bones that join no other bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Held only by muscles and a ligament, it can move up, down, forwards and back. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>
- It forms a base for the tongue above and is attached to the larynx below. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>
- Its movements are coordinated with the tongue, larynx and pharynx in swallowing and speaking. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

*Simpler.* It hangs in the neck on muscles and helps you swallow and talk. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *organ* **Tongue.** The hyoid is its base. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>
- *organ* **Larynx.** Attached below it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>
- *organ* **Pharynx.** Attached behind it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>
- *bone* **Styloid process of the temporal bone.** Gives off a ligament that supports the hyoid. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>
- *muscles* **Small muscles of the neck.** Attach from above and below, moving it up, down, forwards and back. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

*Simpler.* The tongue sits on it, and the larynx and pharynx are attached to it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms from cartilage of the second and third pharyngeal arches. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Strong pressure on the front of the neck, as in strangulation.  
   **Damaged:** The hyoid bone may break.  
   **Patient can’t:** Swallow or speak without pain.  
   **Why:** The hyoid moves with the tongue, larynx and pharynx in swallowing and speaking. † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933; † A fracture of the hyoid is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); its role in swallowing and speech is.]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Weakness of the small muscles attached to the hyoid, for example after a stroke.  
   **Damaged:** The muscles that move the hyoid.  
   **Patient can’t:** Swallow safely: food may enter the airway.  
   **Why:** Swallowing needs the hyoid to move in step with the larynx and pharynx. † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933; † Swallowing problems after a stroke are not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the hyoid’s role is.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the hyoid can move, although it touches no other bone.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>

   - It is held in position by small muscles that attach from above and below.
   - A ligament from the styloid process also supports it.
   - With no joint to another bone, it is free to move.
   - The muscles move it up, down, forwards and back.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the hyoid bone with the bones of the skull.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899464; 9.2 ¶fs-id2663393; 7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

   - The 22 skull bones are joined; 21 are united by sutures, and the mandible moves at its joint.
   - The hyoid touches no other bone.
   - So it is not part of the skull.
   - It is held in place by muscles and a ligament instead.

3. *Predict.* **Place your fingers under your jaw and swallow. Predict what you feel, and explain why.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

   - The hyoid moves.
   - Muscles attached to it pull it during swallowing.
   - It moves in step with the tongue, larynx and pharynx.
   - The larynx, attached below it, moves too.

### Check yourself

1. Which bone does the hyoid touch?  
   A. The mandible · **B. None** · C. The temporal bone · D. The first cervical vertebra  
   *It is an independent bone that does not contact any other bone.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

2. What shape is the hyoid bone?  
   **A. U-shaped** · B. S-shaped · C. Triangular · D. Ring-shaped  
   *It is a small U-shaped bone.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

3. What does the hyoid form a base for?  
   A. The larynx · B. The pharynx · C. The thyroid gland · **D. The tongue**  
   *It serves as the base for the tongue above; the larynx and pharynx are attached to it.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399933]</sub>

---

## 19. Inferior nasal concha

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 7.5 · id `inferior-nasal-concha` · bone `inferior_nasal_concha_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A curved plate of bone projecting into the nasal cavity from its lower side wall (inferior nasal concha). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The inferior nasal concha is a curved shelf of bone inside your nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818]</sub>

*Deeper.* Unlike the other two conchae, it is a separate bone, not part of the ethmoid. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It projects from the lower part of each side wall of the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818]</sub>
- The middle concha, part of the ethmoid, lies just above it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1211287]</sub>
- It is paired: one on each side. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818; 7.2 ¶fs-id1861377]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sticks out from the side wall, low down inside your nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818]</sub>

*Deeper.* The tear drain (nasolacrimal duct) opens into the nasal cavity behind it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Curved plate.** Curves downwards as it projects into the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818; 7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>
- **Size.** The largest of the three nasal conchae. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818]</sub>
- **Attached edge.** Fixed to the lower part of the side wall of the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818]</sub>
- **Visible from the front.** It can easily be seen looking into the front opening of the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a curved shelf, the biggest of three on each side of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818; 7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

*Deeper.* The conchae are named for their positions: superior, middle and inferior. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Its downward curve swirls incoming air, helping to warm and moisten it before it reaches the lungs. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>
- Mucus on its lining traps dust, pollen, bacteria and viruses. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

*Simpler.* It stirs the air you breathe, so the air is warmed, moistened and cleaned. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Middle nasal concha.** Part of the ethmoid bone, just above it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1211287; 7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>
- *bone* **Ethmoid bone.** Forms the superior and middle conchae. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>
- *duct* **Nasolacrimal duct.** Opens into the nasal cavity behind it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>
- *space* **Nasal cavity.** It projects into the lower part of each side. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits below the ethmoid’s conchae, with the tear drain opening behind it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747; 7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

### 6. Development

- The inferior concha forms from a cartilage model (endochondral ossification). † <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id2251103; † OpenStax 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; that this applies to the inferior nasal concha needs an approved source.]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Heavy crying.  
   **Damaged:** Nothing is damaged: the tear drain behind the inferior concha is overloaded.  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep the nose dry.  
   **Why:** Extra tears entering the nasal cavity behind the concha also drain forwards. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A cold or an allergy.  
   **Damaged:** The lining over the conchae swells.  
   **Patient can’t:** Breathe easily through the nose.  
   **Why:** The swollen lining narrows the air passages around the conchae. † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747; † Swelling of the lining narrowing the nasal passages is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the nasal conchae prepare the air you breathe.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

   - They are curved plates projecting into the nasal cavity.
   - They swirl the incoming air.
   - This helps warm and moisten it.
   - Mucus on them traps dust, pollen, bacteria and viruses.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the inferior nasal concha with the middle and superior conchae.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

   - The inferior concha is a separate bone; the other two are part of the ethmoid.
   - It is the largest; the superior is the smallest.
   - The inferior and middle conchae can be seen from the front.
   - The superior concha is hidden above and behind the middle one.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what happens to dust you breathe in through your nose.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

   - The conchae swirl the incoming air.
   - Dust meets the mucus on their lining.
   - It is trapped there.
   - So cleaner, warmer, moister air moves on towards the lungs.

### Check yourself

1. Which nasal concha is a separate bone?  
   A. Superior · B. Middle · **C. Inferior** · D. None of them  
   *The inferior nasal concha is an independent bone of the skull.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

2. Which is the largest nasal concha?  
   **A. Inferior** · B. Middle · C. Superior · D. They are the same size  
   *The inferior concha is the largest.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1698818]</sub>

3. What do the nasal conchae do to incoming air?  
   A. Cool and dry it · B. Remove oxygen from it · C. Speed it up · **D. Swirl it, so it is warmed and moistened**  
   *They swirl the air, which helps warm and moisturise it.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1475747]</sub>

---

## 20. Kidneys

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 25.2 Gross Anatomy of Urine Transport; 25.3 Gross Anatomy of the Kidney; 25.10; 17.10 · id `kidneys` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**Two bean-shaped organs at the back of the abdomen that filter the blood and make urine. † <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1542887; 25.3 ¶fs-id2532837; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The kidneys’ bean shape.]</sub>**

*Simpler.* Your kidneys clean your blood and turn the waste into urine. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id2532837]</sub>

*Deeper.* They also help control blood pressure, make erythropoietin and activate vitamin D. <sub>[25.10 ¶fs-id2844948; 17.10 ¶fs-id1389903]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- They lie either side of the spine, behind the peritoneum, protected by muscle, fat and ribs. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1542887]</sub>
- The left kidney is at about T12 to L3; the right is lower, pushed down by the liver. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1592078]</sub>

*Simpler.* They sit against your back muscles, just under your lowest ribs. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1592078]</sub>

*Deeper.* The eleventh and twelfth ribs partly protect their upper portions. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1592078]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Cortex.** The lighter outer region, holding all the renal corpuscles and convoluted tubules. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id2303293; 25.3 ¶fs-id2506359]</sub>
- **Medulla.** The inner region, with renal pyramids and papillae. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id2303293]</sub>
- **Renal pelvis.** The funnel formed from the calyces; it narrows into the ureter. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1764618; 25.2 ¶fs-id2338701]</sub>
- **Hilum.** Where vessels, nerves, lymphatics and the ureter enter and leave. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1764618]</sub>
- **Capsule and fat.** A fibrous capsule, a cushioning fat pad and renal fascia surround each kidney. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1592078]</sub>

*Simpler.* An outer cortex, an inner medulla and a funnel that collects the urine. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id2303293; 25.3 ¶fs-id1764618]</sub>

*Deeper.* About 1.3 million nephrons in each kidney do the filtering. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id2718604]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Each nephron starts with a glomerulus, a tuft of high-pressure capillaries inside Bowman’s capsule. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id2532837]</sub>
- They receive about 25 percent of the cardiac output at rest. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1542887]</sub>
- Smooth muscle in the renal pelvis and ureter moves urine on by peristalsis. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1764618; 25.2 ¶fs-id2140392]</sub>

*Simpler.* Lots of blood flows through tiny filters, and muscle squeezes the urine down to the bladder. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1542887; 25.3 ¶fs-id2532837; 25.2 ¶fs-id2140392]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *gland* **Adrenal gland.** Sits on top of each kidney; its aldosterone makes the kidney keep sodium. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id2488605]</sub>
- *artery* **Renal artery.** Comes straight from the aorta. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1764618]</sub>
- *vein* **Renal vein.** Returns cleansed blood straight to the inferior vena cava. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1764618]</sub>
- *duct* **Ureter.** About 30 cm long; carries urine from the renal pelvis to the bladder. <sub>[25.2 ¶fs-id2140392; 25.2 ¶fs-id2338701]</sub>
- *organ* **Liver.** Pushes the right kidney a little lower than the left. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1592078]</sub>

*Simpler.* Blood comes from the aorta and goes back to the vena cava; urine goes down the ureter. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1764618]</sub>

### 6. Development

- They develop in the pelvis and rise to their adult position in the abdomen. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The development of the kidneys.]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- The male kidney is usually a little larger than the female. <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1542887]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Kidney failure.  
   **Damaged:** The kidneys’ filtering and their hormone roles.  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep enough red blood cells, or absorb calcium normally.  
   **Why:** Failing kidneys make too little erythropoietin and active vitamin D. <sub>[25.10 ¶fs-id2844948; 25.10 ¶fs-id1586933]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Glomeruli damaged by an autoimmune illness.  
   **Damaged:** The glomerular filter, which lets protein through.  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep protein in the blood: it is lost in the urine, and oedema spreads.  
   **Why:** Low plasma protein lets water move out of the blood into the tissues. <sub>[25.10 ¶fs-id2096572]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the right kidney lies lower than the left.** <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1592078]</sub>

   - Both lie behind the peritoneum either side of the spine.
   - The liver sits above the right kidney.
   - It displaces the right kidney slightly downwards.
   - So the left is at about T12 to L3 and the right a little lower.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the renal cortex and medulla.** <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id2303293; 25.3 ¶fs-id2506359]</sub>

   - The cortex is outer and lighter.
   - It holds the renal corpuscles and convoluted tubules.
   - The medulla is inner, with pyramids and papillae.
   - Its papillae carry urine to the calyces.

3. *Predict.* **Predict a blood change in long-term kidney failure.** † <sub>[17.10 ¶fs-id1918548; 25.10 ¶fs-id2844948; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Anaemia in kidney failure.]</sub>

   - The kidneys make erythropoietin.
   - Erythropoietin stimulates red blood cell formation.
   - Failing kidneys make too little of it.
   - So red blood cells fall (anaemia).

### Check yourself

1. At about which vertebral levels is the left kidney?  
   **A. T12 to L3** · B. T6 to T9 · C. L4 to S1 · D. C7 to T2  
   *The left kidney is at about T12 to L3; the right is lower.* <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id1592078]</sub>

2. Which hormone do the kidneys make that stimulates red blood cell formation?  
   A. Renin · B. Aldosterone · C. Calcitonin · **D. Erythropoietin**  
   *The kidneys produce erythropoietin (EPO).* <sub>[17.10 ¶fs-id1918548]</sub>

3. Where are the renal corpuscles?  
   A. Medulla · B. Renal pelvis · **C. Cortex** · D. Papillae  
   *All renal corpuscles lie in the cortex.* <sub>[25.3 ¶fs-id2506359]</sub>

---

## 21. Lacrimal bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 6.4 · id `lacrimal` · bone `lacrimal_bone_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A small rectangular bone at the front of the inner wall of the orbit (lacrimal bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The lacrimal bone is a tiny bone at the inner corner of your eye socket. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

*Deeper.* Tears drain from the eye through a canal beside it into the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It forms the front part of the medial wall of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>
- With the ethmoid bone, it makes up much of the medial wall. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- It is paired: one in each orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1861377]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits at the inner corner of each eye socket. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its canal opens into the nasal cavity, behind the inferior nasal concha. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Shape.** A small, rectangular plate of bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>
- **Lacrimal fossa.** A shallow hollow in its front part. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>
- **Nasolacrimal canal.** Runs down from the fossa towards the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>
- **Orbital surface.** Forms the front of the medial wall of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a tiny plate of bone with a hollow and a canal for tears. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

*Deeper.* Tears drain into the canal at the inner (medial) corner of the eye. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Tears keep the surface of the eye moist, then drain at the inner corner into its canal. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>
- The duct carries them down into the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>
- With a flood of tears, some fluid drains forwards in the nose, causing a runny nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

*Simpler.* It holds the start of the drain that takes tears from your eye into your nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Ethmoid bone.** Behind it; together they form much of the medial wall of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- *duct* **Nasolacrimal duct.** Carries tears from the eye down into the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>
- *bone* **Inferior nasal concha.** The duct opens into the nasal cavity behind it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>
- *space* **Orbit.** Forms the front of its medial wall. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits beside the ethmoid bone in the eye socket, at the top of the tear drain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927; 7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification). † <sub>[6.4 ¶fs-id1804592; † OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the lacrimal bone needs an approved source.]</sub>
- The flat bones of the face are the last to ossify from membrane. They reach adult size at the end of the adolescent growth spurt. <sub>[6.4 ¶fs-id1639567]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Crying, or an irritated eye, increases the flow of tears.  
   **Damaged:** Nothing is damaged: the tear drain is working, but overloaded.  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep the nose dry: tears run forwards out of the nose.  
   **Why:** Tears normally drain backwards in the nasal cavity; extra fluid also drains forwards. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A baby’s tear duct has not fully opened.  
   **Damaged:** The nasolacrimal duct is blocked.  
   **Patient can’t:** Drain tears: the eye waters all the time.  
   **Why:** Tears can leave the eye only through this duct into the nose. † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176; † A blocked tear duct in babies is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the duct’s course is.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why crying gives you a runny nose.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

   - Tears drain at the inner corner of the eye into the nasolacrimal canal.
   - The duct opens into the nasal cavity, behind the inferior nasal concha.
   - Normally the fluid drains backwards.
   - With many tears, some also drains forwards and out of the nose.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the lacrimal and ethmoid bones in the medial wall of the orbit.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927; 7.2 ¶fs-id886176; 7.2 ¶fs-id1897403]</sub>

   - Both form much of the medial wall.
   - The lacrimal bone is small and lies in front.
   - The ethmoid lies behind it, between the orbit and the nasal cavity.
   - The ethmoid contains air cells; the lacrimal bone holds the tear canal.

3. *Predict.* **Predict where eye drops go after you blink.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

   - They drain at the inner corner of the eye.
   - They pass into the nasolacrimal canal, beside the lacrimal bone.
   - The duct carries them down into the nasal cavity.
   - They drain backwards in the nose.

### Check yourself

1. Where does the nasolacrimal duct open?  
   A. Into the mouth · **B. Into the nasal cavity** · C. Into the orbit · D. Into the maxillary sinus  
   *It opens into the nasal cavity, behind the inferior nasal concha.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

2. Which part of the orbit does the lacrimal bone form?  
   **A. The front of the medial wall** · B. The roof · C. The lateral wall · D. The floor  
   *It forms the anterior, medial wall of the orbit.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

3. Why does crying give you a runny nose?  
   A. The nasal bones swell · B. The sinuses fill · C. Mucus is made faster · **D. Extra tears drain forwards in the nasal cavity**  
   *With increased tears, some fluid drains anteriorly in the nasal cavity.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id886176]</sub>

---

## 22. Liver

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 23.6 Accessory Organs in Digestion: The Liver, Pancreas, and Gallbladder · id `liver` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The largest gland in the body: it makes bile and processes the nutrients and toxins absorbed from the gut. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1907412; 23.6 ¶fs-id1966736; glossary: liver]</sub>**

*Simpler.* Your liver is a big organ under your right ribs. It makes bile and cleans your blood. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1907412; 23.6 ¶fs-id1966736]</sub>

*Deeper.* Besides digestion, it has many roles in metabolism and regulation. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1907412]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It lies below the diaphragm in the right upper quadrant of the abdomen, protected by the ribs. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1907412]</sub>
- Peritoneal folds called ligaments attach it to the diaphragm and the abdominal wall. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1479465]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits high on the right side of your abdomen, under your ribs. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1907412]</sub>

*Deeper.* The lesser omentum tethers it to the lesser curvature of the stomach. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1479465]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Right and left lobes.** A large right lobe and a much smaller left lobe. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1479465]</sub>
- **Quadrate and caudate lobes.** Smaller lobes some anatomists identify within the right lobe. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1479465]</sub>
- **Falciform ligament.** A remnant of the umbilical vein that separates the right and left lobes in front. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1479465]</sub>
- **Porta hepatis.** The “gate” where the hepatic artery and hepatic portal vein enter. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1842507]</sub>

*Simpler.* Two main lobes, with vessels entering at a “gate” underneath. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1479465; 23.6 ¶fs-id1842507]</sub>

*Deeper.* It weighs about three pounds (about 1.4 kg) in an adult. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1907412]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Hepatocytes, about 80 percent of its volume, line porous sinusoids and process what the blood brings. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1257384; 23.6 ¶fs-id1966736]</sub>
- It makes about one litre of bile a day; bile salts emulsify fats. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1386125; 23.6 ¶fs-id1390455]</sub>
- Bile salts are reclaimed in the ileum and recycled (the enterohepatic circulation). <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1216217]</sub>

*Simpler.* Its cells sit right against the blood, clean it, and make bile to break up fats. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1966736; 23.6 ¶fs-id1390455]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *vein* **Hepatic portal vein.** The hepatic portal vein brings nutrient-rich blood from the gut. It supplies more oxygen than the hepatic artery. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1842507]</sub>
- *artery* **Hepatic artery.** Brings oxygenated blood from the heart. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1842507]</sub>
- *organ* **Gallbladder.** Stores and concentrates the bile between meals. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1379189; 23.6 ¶fs-id2279526]</sub>
- *duct* **Common bile duct.** Formed by the common hepatic and cystic ducts; carries bile to the duodenum. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1356003]</sub>
- *muscle* **Diaphragm.** Lies directly above it. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1907412]</sub>

*Simpler.* Blood comes in from the gut and the heart; bile goes out to the gallbladder and duodenum. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1842507; 23.6 ¶fs-id1356003]</sub>

### 6. Development

- The falciform ligament and the ligamentum teres hepatis are remnants of the fetal umbilical vein. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1479465]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- Some anatomists divide the right lobe further into quadrate and caudate lobes. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1479465]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A blockage that stops bile reaching the intestine.  
   **Damaged:** The flow of bile into the duodenum.  
   **Patient can’t:** Digest fats normally: the stool is pale (acholic) and fatty.  
   **Why:** Without bile, fats are not emulsified and bilirubin does not colour the stool. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1884700; 23.6 ¶fs-id1390455]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A build-up of bilirubin when red cell breakdown or bile flow goes wrong.  
   **Damaged:** The liver’s excretion of bilirubin into bile.  
   **Patient can’t:** Clear bilirubin: the skin and eyes turn yellow (jaundice).  
   **Why:** Bilirubin is a waste of old red cells that the liver normally excretes in bile. † <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1884700; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Jaundice and its causes.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how bile helps digest fats.** <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1390455; 23.6 ¶fs-id1386125]</sub>

   - Fats do not dissolve in the watery chyme.
   - Bile salts have a water-loving and a fat-loving region.
   - They pull large fat globules apart into droplets about 1 micrometre across.
   - This emulsification gives lipase a larger surface to act on.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the blood the liver receives from the hepatic artery and the hepatic portal vein.** <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1842507]</sub>

   - The hepatic artery brings oxygenated blood from the heart.
   - The portal vein brings partly deoxygenated, nutrient-rich blood from the gut.
   - The portal vein also carries absorbed drugs and toxins.
   - It actually supplies more oxygen than the smaller hepatic arteries.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what happens to bile between meals.** <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1379189]</sub>

   - The liver keeps making bile.
   - The hepatopancreatic ampulla closes.
   - Bile is diverted into the gallbladder.
   - There it is concentrated and stored until the next meal.

### Check yourself

1. Where is the liver?  
   A. Left upper quadrant · **B. Right upper quadrant** · C. Behind the stomach in the retroperitoneum · D. In the pelvis  
   *It lies inferior to the diaphragm in the right upper quadrant.* <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1907412]</sub>

2. What enters the liver at the porta hepatis?  
   **A. Hepatic artery and hepatic portal vein** · B. Hepatic veins · C. Inferior vena cava · D. Cystic duct  
   *The hepatic artery and hepatic portal vein enter at the porta hepatis.* <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1842507]</sub>

3. What gives faeces their brown colour?  
   A. Bile salts · B. Cholesterol · C. Lecithin · **D. Stercobilin, from bilirubin**  
   *Intestinal bacteria turn bilirubin into stercobilin, a brown pigment.* <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1884700]</sub>
