## 23. Lunate

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (and glossary); 8.5; 9.4 · id `lunate` · bone `lunate_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A moon-shaped wrist bone in the middle of the first row of carpal bones (lunate). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: lunate]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The lunate is a small wrist bone in the middle of your wrist. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

*Deeper.* With the scaphoid, it is one of only two carpal bones that meet the radius directly. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It is the second bone of the first (proximal) row, counting from the thumb side. <sub>[glossary: lunate; 8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- Above it is the radius. Below it are the capitate and hamate. <sub>[glossary: lunate]</sub>
- The scaphoid lies beside it on the thumb side, and the triquetrum on the little-finger side. <sub>[glossary: lunate]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits in the middle of the wrist, just below the end of the radius. <sub>[glossary: lunate]</sub>

*Deeper.* Only three carpal bones take part in the wrist joint: the scaphoid, lunate and triquetrum. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Moon shape.** “Lunate” means moon-shaped. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- **Surface for the radius.** Its upper surface meets the radius directly at the wrist joint. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>
- **Surfaces for the second row.** Its lower surface meets the capitate and hamate at the midcarpal joint. <sub>[glossary: lunate; 8.2 ¶fs-id932152]</sub>
- **Side surfaces.** It meets the scaphoid on the thumb side and the triquetrum on the other. <sub>[glossary: lunate]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a small curved bone with joints on four sides. <sub>[glossary: lunate]</sub>

*Deeper.* Ligaments unite it with the other three bones of its row, so they work as one unit. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Its upper surface meets the radius, so it shares the wrist joint with the scaphoid. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>
- Its lower surface takes part in the midcarpal joint. The two joints together make all wrist movements. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id932152]</sub>
- With the other carpal bones it forms the walls and floor of the carpal tunnel. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is one of the bones the wrist bends on. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id932152]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joint* **Wrist (radiocarpal) joint.** A condyloid joint: the lunate and scaphoid meet the radius. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1434553; 8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>
- *joint* **Midcarpal joint.** Its lower surface meets the capitate and hamate. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id932152; glossary: lunate]</sub>
- *bones* **Scaphoid and triquetrum.** Its neighbours in the first row, joined to it by ligaments. <sub>[glossary: lunate; 8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>
- *space* **Carpal tunnel.** The carpal bones form its walls and floor. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>
- *nerve* **Median nerve.** Runs through the carpal tunnel, in front of the lunate. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

*Simpler.* It joins the radius above, the second row below and its two neighbours at the sides. <sub>[glossary: lunate]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification), like all limb bones except the clavicle. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Its single ossification centre appears at about the fifth year. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives the fifth year (supporting only).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Overuse of the tendons or a wrist injury makes the contents of the carpal tunnel swell.  
   **Damaged:** The nerve in the tunnel, whose walls and floor the carpal bones form.  
   **Patient can’t:** Use the hand without pain, numbness or weakness (carpal tunnel syndrome).  
   **Why:** The bony walls and floor and the tough roof cannot stretch, so swelling presses on the nerve. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A fall onto the outstretched hand with the wrist bent far back.  
   **Damaged:** The lunate dislocates forwards, into the carpal tunnel.  
   **Patient can’t:** Feel the thumb, index and middle fingers normally.  
   **Why:** The dislocated bone takes up space in the tunnel and presses on the median nerve. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain which carpal bones form the wrist joint, and how.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937; reviewer, 2026-11-25]</sub>

   - Three carpal bones take part: the scaphoid, lunate and triquetrum.
   - The scaphoid and lunate meet the radius directly.
   - The triquetrum meets the articular disc over the ulna.
   - The ulna itself does not touch the carpal bones.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the lunate and the scaphoid.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; 8.2 ¶fs-id1882937; 8.2 ¶fs-id2637051]</sub>

   - Both are in the first row, and both meet the radius directly.
   - The scaphoid is boat-shaped and outermost, on the thumb side.
   - The lunate is moon-shaped and second from the thumb side.
   - The scaphoid is the carpal bone broken most often.

3. *Predict.* **Predict the effect of the lunate dislocating forwards into the carpal tunnel.** † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

   - It takes up space in the tunnel.
   - It presses on the median nerve.
   - Numbness of the thumb, index and middle fingers.
   - Weakness of the small muscles of the thumb.

### Check yourself

1. Which bones meet the lower surface of the lunate?  
   **A. Capitate and hamate** · B. The radius · C. Trapezium and trapezoid · D. The pisiform  
   *Its lower surface meets the capitate and hamate at the midcarpal joint.* <sub>[glossary: lunate]</sub>

2. What does “lunate” mean?  
   A. Boat-shaped · **B. Moon-shaped** · C. Pea-shaped · D. Hooked  
   *“Lunate” means moon-shaped.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

3. Which two carpal bones meet the radius directly?  
   A. Triquetrum and pisiform · B. Capitate and hamate · C. Trapezium and trapezoid · **D. Scaphoid and lunate**  
   *The scaphoid and lunate meet the radius; the triquetrum meets the articular disc over the ulna.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937; reviewer, 2026-11-25]</sub>

## 24. Lungs

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 22.1 Organs and Structures of the Respiratory System; 22.2 The Lungs; 22.3; 22.7 · id `lungs` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The paired, pyramid-shaped organs of gas exchange in the chest, enclosed by the pleurae. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237; 22.2 ¶fs-id1401100]</sub>**

*Simpler.* Your lungs take oxygen from the air into your blood and pass carbon dioxide out. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id1401100]</sub>

*Deeper.* They exchange gases across about 70 square metres of very thin, highly permeable epithelium. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id1401100]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- They fill the thoracic cavity on either side of the mediastinum, and rest on the diaphragm. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237; 22.2 ¶fs-id2346435]</sub>
- Each connects to the trachea by its main bronchus. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237]</sub>

*Simpler.* They fill most of your chest, either side of your heart. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237]</sub>

*Deeper.* The apex is at the top; the base rests on the diaphragm. The costal surface faces the ribs. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Lobes.** The right lung has three lobes; the left lung has two. Fissures separate them. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2717313]</sub>
- **Cardiac notch.** An indentation in the left lung that makes room for the heart. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237]</sub>
- **Bronchopulmonary segments.** Divisions of a lobe, each with its own tertiary bronchus and artery. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2717313]</sub>
- **Alveoli.** Tiny grape-like air sacs, about 200 micrometres across, with elastic walls. <sub>[22.1 ¶fs-id2594216]</sub>
- **Hilum.** Where the nerves enter the lung root and the two layers of pleura meet. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2292208; 22.2 ¶fs-id2346435]</sub>

*Simpler.* Three lobes on the right, two on the left, branching down to millions of tiny air sacs. † <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2717313; 22.1 ¶fs-id2594216; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The number of alveoli (“millions”).]</sub>

*Deeper.* The right lung is shorter and wider; the left holds a smaller volume, sharing space with the heart. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Alveolar and capillary walls together form a respiratory membrane about 0.5 micrometres thick. <sub>[22.1 ¶fs-id2488371]</sub>
- Type II alveolar cells secrete surfactant, which lowers surface tension, so the alveoli do not collapse. <sub>[22.1 ¶fs-id2201617; 22.3 ¶fs-id1862104]</sub>
- The lungs are passive in breathing: pleural fluid makes them follow the chest wall as it moves. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2078585; 22.3 ¶fs-id1882180]</sub>

*Simpler.* Very thin walls let gases pass quickly; a slippery film keeps the air sacs open. <sub>[22.1 ¶fs-id2488371; 22.3 ¶fs-id1862104]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *membrane* **Pleura.** Visceral pleura covers the lung; parietal pleura lines the chest wall. Pleural fluid lies between. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2346435; 22.2 ¶fs-id2078585]</sub>
- *muscle* **Diaphragm.** Borders the lungs below; its contraction enlarges the chest and draws air in. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237; 22.3 ¶fs-id1882180]</sub>
- *artery* **Pulmonary arteries.** Bring deoxygenated blood from the pulmonary trunk to the alveoli. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2144132]</sub>
- *nerve* **Autonomic nerves.** Parasympathetic nerves constrict the airways; sympathetic nerves dilate them. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2292208]</sub>
- *airway* **Bronchi.** Connect each lung to the trachea and branch within it. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237; 22.2 ¶fs-id2717313]</sub>

*Simpler.* They sit on the diaphragm, wrapped in pleura, fed by the pulmonary arteries. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237; 22.2 ¶fs-id2346435; 22.2 ¶fs-id2144132]</sub>

### 6. Development

- The lung bud grows from the foregut at about week 4; most respiratory structures arise from endoderm. <sub>[22.7 ¶fs-id2485319; 22.7 ¶fs-id2486303]</sub>
- By week 28 enough alveoli have matured that a premature baby can usually breathe alone. <sub>[22.7 ¶fs-id2287234]</sub>
- Surfactant is usually not adequate until about the eighth month of pregnancy. <sub>[22.7 ¶fs-id1910265]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- Second-hand smoke raises a non-smoker’s risk of lung cancer by up to 30 percent. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2573116]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A baby born before enough surfactant is made.  
   **Damaged:** The alveoli, whose surface tension is too high.  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep the alveoli open when breathing out.  
   **Why:** Without surfactant, the alveoli collapse during expiration. <sub>[22.3 ¶fs-id1862104; 22.7 ¶fs-id1910265]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A disease confined to one bronchopulmonary segment.  
   **Damaged:** That segment only.  
   **Patient can’t:** Use that segment for gas exchange.  
   **Why:** Each segment has its own bronchus and artery, so it can be removed with little effect on its neighbours. <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2717313]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the lungs expand though they have no muscle to move them.** <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2078585; 22.3 ¶fs-id1882180]</sub>

   - The diaphragm and intercostals enlarge the thoracic cavity.
   - Pleural fluid holds the lungs to the chest wall by surface tension.
   - So the lungs are pulled outwards and stretch.
   - Their pressure falls, and air flows in.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the right and left lungs.** <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2717313; 22.2 ¶fs-id2627237]</sub>

   - Right: three lobes (superior, middle, inferior).
   - Left: two lobes (superior, inferior).
   - The right is shorter and wider.
   - The left is smaller, with a cardiac notch for the heart.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what happens to a baby born at 26 weeks without treatment.** † <sub>[22.7 ¶fs-id1910265; 22.3 ¶fs-id1862104; 22.7 ¶fs-id2287234; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The effort of breathing without surfactant (respiratory distress in premature babies).]</sub>

   - Its type II cells make little surfactant.
   - Surface tension in the alveoli stays high.
   - The alveoli collapse when it breathes out.
   - So each breath takes great effort.

### Check yourself

1. How many lobes does the left lung have?  
   A. One · **B. Two** · C. Three · D. Four  
   *The left lung has two lobes, superior and inferior; the right has three.* <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2717313]</sub>

2. Which cells secrete pulmonary surfactant?  
   A. Type I alveolar cells · B. Alveolar macrophages · **C. Type II alveolar cells** · D. Goblet cells  
   *Type II alveolar cells secrete surfactant.* <sub>[22.1 ¶fs-id2201617]</sub>

3. What is the cardiac notch?  
   **A. An indentation of the left lung for the heart** · B. A fissure of the right lung · C. The hilum of the left lung · D. A groove for the aorta  
   *It is an indentation on the left lung that allows space for the heart.* <sub>[22.2 ¶fs-id2627237]</sub>

---

## 25. Maxilla

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 6.4; 7.5; 9.2 · id `maxilla` · bone `maxilla_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**One of two bones forming the upper jaw, most of the hard palate and part of the orbit floor (maxilla). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The maxilla is your upper jaw bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

*Deeper.* It contains the largest paranasal sinus, the maxillary sinus, just below the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- The two maxillae meet in the midline to form the upper jaw. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253; 7.2 ¶fs-id1845434]</sub>
- Each forms the inner (medial) floor of the orbit and the side of the base of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- Its lower edge holds the upper teeth. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

*Simpler.* It forms your upper jaw, just below the eye. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

*Deeper.* With the palatine bones, it forms the hard palate between the mouth and the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253; 7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Alveolar process.** The curved lower edge that holds the upper teeth. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>
- **Infraorbital foramen.** An opening just below the orbit, for a sensory nerve to the nose, upper lip and front of the cheek. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>
- **Palatine process.** A shelf that meets its partner to form the front three-quarters of the hard palate. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>
- **Orbital surface.** Forms most of the inner floor of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- **Maxillary sinus.** The largest paranasal sinus, below the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

*Simpler.* It has a ridge holding the upper teeth, a shelf forming the roof of the mouth and a large sinus. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

*Deeper.* Each tooth sits in a deep socket called an alveolus. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Its alveolar process anchors the upper teeth in deep sockets. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>
- Its palatine processes meet in the midline, separating the mouth from the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>
- The large maxillary sinus lightens the skull, but its opening is high on the inner wall, so it drains poorly. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1977054; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

*Simpler.* It holds your upper teeth and forms most of the roof of your mouth. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joints* **Upper teeth.** Each tooth root is held in its socket by a fibrous joint (gomphosis). <sub>[9.2 ¶fs-id1747622; 7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>
- *bones* **Palatine bones.** Their horizontal plates complete the back quarter of the hard palate. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>
- *nerve* **Sensory nerve to the face.** Leaves through the infraorbital foramen to supply the nose, upper lip and front of the cheek. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>
- *space* **Orbit.** The maxilla forms most of its inner floor, with a small part from the palatine bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- *space* **Nasal cavity.** The hard palate forms its floor, and the maxilla the side of the base of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

*Simpler.* It holds the upper teeth and joins the palatine bones, the orbit and the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253; 7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

### 6. Development

- The right and left maxillae come together in the midline during embryonic development. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1845434]</sub>
- Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification). † <sub>[6.4 ¶fs-id1804592; † OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the maxilla needs an approved source.]</sub>
- In childhood the jaws grow as the teeth appear, and the sinuses enlarge. <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1241553]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A sinus infection, often after a cold.  
   **Damaged:** The maxillary sinus, the sinus most often infected.  
   **Patient can’t:** Clear the sinus easily: pressure and pain build below the eye.  
   **Why:** The sinus opens into the nasal cavity high on its inner wall, so it drains poorly. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882870; 7.2 ¶fs-id1977054]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A blow to the eye from in front, as from a ball.  
   **Damaged:** The thin floor of the orbit breaks into the maxillary sinus below (blow-out fracture).  
   **Patient can’t:** Look upwards without double vision.  
   **Why:** An eye muscle and orbital fat can be trapped in the break. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the maxillary sinus is the one most often infected.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882870; 7.2 ¶fs-id1977054]</sub>

   - It is the largest paranasal sinus.
   - Its connection to the nasal cavity is high on its inner wall.
   - So it is difficult to drain.
   - Trapped fluid and mucus allow infection and cause pressure and pain.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the parts the maxillae and the palatine bones play in the hard palate.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253; 7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

   - The palatine processes of the maxillae form the front three-quarters.
   - The horizontal plates of the palatine bones form the back quarter.
   - All four meet in the midline.
   - Together they separate the mouth from the nasal cavity.

3. *Predict.* **A sensory nerve leaves the skull through the infraorbital foramen. Predict where a patient is numb if it is injured.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

   - The side of the nose.
   - The upper lip.
   - The front of the cheek.
   - On the same side as the injured nerve.

### Check yourself

1. Which is the largest paranasal sinus?  
   A. Frontal sinus · **B. Maxillary sinus** · C. Sphenoid sinus · D. Ethmoid air cells  
   *The maxillary sinus is the largest.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882870]</sub>

2. Which part of the hard palate do the maxillae form?  
   **A. The front three-quarters** · B. The back quarter · C. The whole palate · D. None of it  
   *Their palatine processes form the anterior three-quarters of the hard palate.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

3. What does the alveolar process of the maxilla hold?  
   A. The eyeball · B. The tongue · C. The nasal septum · **D. The upper teeth**  
   *It contains the sockets of the upper teeth.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

---

## 26. Metacarpal bones

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (and glossary); 8.5; 9.4; 9.5; 20.5 · id `metacarpals` · bone `metacarpal_3_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The five long bones of the palm, between the wrist and the fingers (metacarpal bones). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304; glossary: metacarpal bone]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The metacarpals are the five bones of your palm. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

*Deeper.* They are numbered 1 to 5, starting at the thumb. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- They lie between the carpal bones of the wrist and the bones of the fingers and thumb. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>
- Each base meets a carpal bone of the second row. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>
- Their lower ends form the knuckles, at the base of the fingers. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

*Simpler.* They make up your palm, from your wrist to your knuckles. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

*Deeper.* The first metacarpal, at the base of the thumb, is separate from the others. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Base.** The upper end, which meets a carpal bone at a carpometacarpal joint. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304; glossary: carpometacarpal joint]</sub>
- **Shaft.** The long middle part of each bone. <sub>[glossary: metacarpal bone]</sub>
- **Head.** The expanded lower end, which forms a knuckle. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>
- **First metacarpal.** Separate from the others, at the base of the thumb; its base forms a saddle joint with the trapezium. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751; 9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>
- **Second and third metacarpals.** Firmly anchored, so they do not move. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>
- **Fourth and fifth metacarpals.** Can move a little forwards and backwards; the fifth moves most. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

*Simpler.* Each has a base at the wrist end, a shaft and a head that forms a knuckle. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304; glossary: metacarpal bone]</sub>

*Deeper.* Each head meets the base of a proximal phalanx at a metacarpophalangeal joint. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304; glossary: metacarpophalangeal joint]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- The first metacarpal is separate, so the thumb can move freely on its own. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>
- The second and third are fixed, giving the palm a firm middle. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>
- The fourth and fifth move forwards in a power grip, pressing the inner hand against the object. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

*Simpler.* A free thumb bone, a firm middle and movable outer bones help you grip. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joints* **Carpometacarpal joints.** Between the second row of carpal bones and the bases of the metacarpals. <sub>[glossary: carpometacarpal joint]</sub>
- *joint* **First carpometacarpal joint.** A saddle joint between the trapezium and the first metacarpal. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>
- *joints* **Metacarpophalangeal (knuckle) joints.** Condyloid joints between the metacarpal heads and the proximal phalanges. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1434553]</sub>
- *bones* **Carpal bones.** The trapezium carries the first and second, and the trapezoid the second. The capitate carries mainly the third, and the hamate the fourth and fifth. <sub>[glossary: trapezium; glossary: trapezoid; glossary: capitate; glossary: hamate]</sub>
- *arteries* **Palmar arches.** The radial and ulnar arteries join at the wrist to form the palmar arches that supply the hand. <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id1595346]</sub>
- *veins* **Palmar venous arches.** The digital veins join in the hand to form the palmar venous arches. <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id3239091]</sub>

*Simpler.* They join the wrist bones above and the finger bones below. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

### 6. Development

- They form by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification). <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Each has two ossification centres: one for the shaft, and one for the head (in the first, for the base). † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives two centres for each metacarpal (supporting only).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- The first metacarpal ossifies like a phalanx, so some anatomists count the thumb as having three phalanges. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives two centres for each metacarpal (supporting only).]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A punch that lands on a hard object.  
   **Damaged:** The neck of the fifth metacarpal breaks (“boxer’s fracture”).  
   **Patient can’t:** Make a fist with a normal knuckle: the little-finger knuckle sinks.  
   **Why:** The fifth metacarpal is the most mobile of the palm bones and takes the force of a badly aimed punch. † <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751; † The fracture is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the mobility of the fifth metacarpal is.]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A blow along the bent thumb, as in a punch.  
   **Damaged:** The base of the first metacarpal breaks into the saddle joint (Bennett fracture).  
   **Patient can’t:** Pinch or touch the thumb to the fingertips without pain.  
   **Why:** The break enters the saddle joint on which opposition depends. † <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120; 9.5 ¶fs-id1354267; † The fracture is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the saddle joint and opposition are.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the thumb’s metacarpal moves so much more than the others.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751; 9.4 ¶fs-id1837120; 9.5 ¶fs-id1354267]</sub>

   - The first metacarpal is separate from the other four.
   - Its base forms a saddle joint with the trapezium, which is biaxial.
   - So it moves within the plane of the palm and out in front of it.
   - Flexion with abduction here produces opposition.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the movement of the second and third metacarpals with that of the fourth and fifth.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

   - The second and third are firmly anchored and immobile.
   - The fourth and fifth move a little forwards and backwards.
   - The fifth moves most.
   - Their forward movement strengthens the grip of the inner hand.

3. *Predict.* **A man punches a wall and the knuckle of his little finger sinks. Predict the injury and explain it.** † <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

   - A fracture of the neck of the fifth metacarpal (boxer’s fracture).
   - The head tips towards the palm.
   - So the knuckle sinks when he makes a fist.
   - The fifth metacarpal is the most mobile and least protected.

### Check yourself

1. Which metacarpal is at the base of the thumb?  
   **A. First** · B. Second · C. Third · D. Fifth  
   *The metacarpals are numbered from the thumb: the first is at its base.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

2. Which metacarpals are firmly anchored and do not move?  
   A. First and second · B. Fourth and fifth · **C. Second and third** · D. Third and fourth  
   *The second and third are firmly anchored.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

3. What forms the knuckles?  
   A. The bases of the metacarpals · B. The second row of carpal bones · C. The proximal phalanges · **D. The heads (lower ends) of the metacarpals**  
   *The expanded distal end of each metacarpal forms a knuckle.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

---

## 27. Nasal bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 6.4 · id `nasal` · bone `nasal_bone_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**One of two small bones that form the bridge of the nose (nasal bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The nasal bones form the hard top of your nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

*Deeper.* They also support the cartilages that form the sides of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- The two nasal bones meet in the midline at the bridge of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>
- They form the bony base of the nose, above its cartilages. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>
- They are paired facial bones. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1861377]</sub>

*Simpler.* Feel the hard top of your nose: those are the nasal bones. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

*Deeper.* Below them, the nose is shaped by cartilage, which is not seen in a dry skull. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580; 7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Midline joint.** The two bones meet each other in the midline. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>
- **Bridge of the nose.** Together they form its bony base. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>
- **Lower edges.** Support the cartilages of the side walls of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>
- **Upper and side edges.** Meet the frontal bone above and the maxillae at the sides. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

*Simpler.* They are two small bones side by side at the top of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

*Deeper.* As facial bones, they are joined to their neighbours by sutures. <sub>[9.2 ¶fs-id2663393]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Together they form a firm bridge that holds the shape of the upper nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>
- They support the softer cartilages of the side walls of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

*Simpler.* They give your nose a firm top and hold up its softer parts. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Other nasal bone.** Its partner, meeting it in the midline. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>
- *cartilage* **Cartilages of the nose.** The side-wall cartilages rest on them. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>
- *cartilage* **Septal cartilage.** Forms the front of the nasal septum and separates the nostrils. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>
- *bones* **Frontal bone and maxillae.** Meet them above and at the sides. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

*Simpler.* They meet each other in the midline and hold up the cartilages of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification). † <sub>[6.4 ¶fs-id1804592; † OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the nasal bone needs an approved source.]</sub>
- The flat bones of the face are the last to ossify from membrane. They reach adult size at the end of the adolescent growth spurt. <sub>[6.4 ¶fs-id1639567]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A blow to the nose.  
   **Damaged:** The nasal bones break: these are the bones damaged when the nose is broken.  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep the nose straight: the bridge may be pushed to one side.  
   **Why:** The thin bones form the bony bridge, so a blow drives them sideways or inwards. † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580; † OpenStax says these bones break in a broken nose; displacement of the bridge is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A broken nose with a swollen, blocked nostril.  
   **Damaged:** Blood collects under the lining of the septal cartilage (septal haematoma).  
   **Patient can’t:** Breathe through that side of the nose.  
   **Why:** The cartilage depends on that lining for nourishment, so trapped blood threatens it. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why a broken nose is usually a break of the nasal bones.** † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580; 7.2 ¶fs-id2125356; † That cartilage bends rather than breaks is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

   - The nasal bones form the bony bridge of the nose.
   - They are small and project at the front of the face.
   - The rest of the nose is cartilage, which bends rather than breaks.
   - So a blow breaks the nasal bones.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the bony and the cartilage parts of the nose.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580; 7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

   - Bone: the nasal bones form the bridge.
   - Cartilage: plates form the side walls and the front of the septum.
   - The bones support the side-wall cartilages.
   - Cartilage is not seen in a dry skull.

3. *Predict.* **Predict why a skull in a museum seems to have no nose.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580; 7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

   - Only the nasal bones, at the bridge, are bone.
   - The rest of the nose is cartilage.
   - Cartilage is not found in the dry skull.
   - So a large opening is left below the nasal bones.

### Check yourself

1. How many nasal bones are there?  
   A. One · **B. Two** · C. Three · D. Four  
   *Two small nasal bones join to form the bridge of the nose.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

2. What do the nasal bones support?  
   **A. The cartilages of the side walls of the nose** · B. The upper teeth · C. The eyeball · D. The tongue  
   *They support the cartilages that form the lateral walls of the nose.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

3. Which bones are damaged when the nose is broken?  
   A. Vomer · B. Ethmoid bone · C. Lacrimal bones · **D. Nasal bones**  
   *The nasal bones are damaged when the nose is broken.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1899580]</sub>

## 28. Occipital bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 7.3; 7.5; 13.2; 16.3; 20.5 · id `occipital` · bone `occipital_bone` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The single bone of the back and base of the skull, around the foramen magnum (occipital bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The occipital bone is the bone at the back of your head. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its condyles rest on the first cervical vertebra, so the skull sits on the vertebral column. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275; 7.3 ¶fs-id2228247]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It forms the back of the skull and the back of the base of the cranial cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>
- It meets the two parietal bones and the temporal bones at the lambdoid suture. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- Inside, it forms the floor and back wall of the posterior cranial fossa. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1489044]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is at the back of your head, down to where your skull sits on your neck. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>

*Deeper.* The posterior cranial fossa is the deepest of the three, and holds the cerebellum. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1489044]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **External occipital protuberance.** A small bump at the back midline, where a ligament of the neck attaches. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>
- **Superior nuchal lines.** Lines either side of the bump: the highest point where neck muscles attach. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>
- **Foramen magnum.** The large opening (“great aperture”) in the base, for the spinal cord. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275; 7.2 ¶fs-id1489044]</sub>
- **Occipital condyles.** Oval knobs either side of the foramen magnum, which form joints with the first cervical vertebra. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>
- **Hypoglossal canals.** Openings at the front edge of the foramen magnum, for a nerve to the tongue. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1724550]</sub>
- **Venous grooves.** Large curved grooves on the inner wall, ending at the jugular foramen. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1391977]</sub>

*Simpler.* It has a bump at the back, a large hole underneath and two knobs that sit on the spine. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>

*Deeper.* Above the nuchal lines only the scalp covers the skull; below them, neck muscles attach. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Its condyles form joints with the first cervical vertebra, supporting the skull on the spine. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275; 7.3 ¶fs-id2228247]</sub>
- The foramen magnum lets the spinal cord pass out of the skull. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>
- Its protuberance and nuchal lines anchor a ligament and the muscles of the back of the neck. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>

*Simpler.* It holds your head on your neck and lets the spinal cord out. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **First cervical vertebra (atlas).** Supports the skull: it forms joints with the occipital condyles. <sub>[7.3 ¶fs-id2228247; 7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>
- *nervous system* **Spinal cord.** Leaves the skull through the foramen magnum. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>
- *brain* **Cerebellum.** Lies in the posterior cranial fossa. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1489044]</sub>
- *arteries* **Vertebral arteries.** Enter the skull through the foramen magnum to supply the brain and spinal cord. <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id2009844]</sub>
- *nerve* **Hypoglossal nerve.** The motor nerve of the tongue; it passes through the hypoglossal canal. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1724550; 16.3 ¶fs-id1865012]</sub>
- *suture* **Lambdoid suture.** Joins it to the parietal and temporal bones. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits on the first neck vertebra and lets the spinal cord and two arteries through. <sub>[7.3 ¶fs-id2228247; 7.2 ¶fs-id1478275; 20.5 ¶fs-id2009844]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Its upper part, behind the brain, forms from membrane; its base forms from a cartilage model. † <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1547037; 7.5 ¶fs-id2251103; † OpenStax says the top and sides of the brain case form from membrane, and its floor from cartilage; how this divides the occipital bone needs an approved source.]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A blow to the front or side of the head.  
   **Damaged:** The base of the occipital bone may break on the opposite side (contrecoup), a basilar fracture.  
   **Patient can’t:** Rely on the site of the blow to show where the skull broke.  
   **Why:** In a contrecoup fracture, the bone breaks opposite the point of impact. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1972896]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Bleeding or swelling raises the pressure inside the skull.  
   **Damaged:** The lowest part of the cerebellum is pushed down through the foramen magnum.  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep breathing and heart rate regulated: the brainstem is compressed.  
   **Why:** The pons and medulla, at the foramen magnum, regulate breathing and the heart. † <sub>[13.2 ¶fs-id1523364; † Herniation through the foramen magnum is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the brainstem’s role is.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the skull is supported on the vertebral column.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275; 7.3 ¶fs-id2228247]</sub>

   - The occipital bone has an oval condyle on each side of the foramen magnum.
   - The condyles form joints with the first cervical vertebra.
   - This vertebra is called the atlas because it supports the skull.
   - So the skull rests on top of the vertebral column.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the foramen magnum and the hypoglossal canal.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275; 20.5 ¶fs-id2009844; 7.2 ¶fs-id1724550; 16.3 ¶fs-id1865012]</sub>

   - The foramen magnum is the large opening in the base of the occipital bone.
   - It carries the spinal cord and the vertebral arteries.
   - The hypoglossal canal is a small opening at its front edge.
   - It carries the hypoglossal nerve, the motor nerve of the tongue.

3. *Predict.* **Predict why a blow to the forehead can break the occipital bone.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1972896]</sub>

   - This is a contrecoup (counterblow) fracture.
   - The bone at the point of impact does not break.
   - Instead, the skull breaks on the opposite side.
   - At the base of the skull this gives a basilar fracture.

### Check yourself

1. What passes through the foramen magnum?  
   A. The optic nerve · **B. The spinal cord** · C. The facial nerve · D. The internal carotid artery  
   *The foramen magnum allows passage of the spinal cord as it exits the skull.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275]</sub>

2. Which bone do the occipital condyles meet?  
   A. Axis (second cervical vertebra) · B. Sphenoid bone · **C. Atlas (first cervical vertebra)** · D. Temporal bone  
   *They form joints with the first cervical vertebra, the atlas.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1478275; 7.3 ¶fs-id2228247]</sub>

3. Which part of the brain lies in the posterior cranial fossa?  
   A. Frontal lobes · B. Temporal lobes · C. Pituitary gland · **D. Cerebellum**  
   *The posterior cranial fossa contains the cerebellum.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1489044]</sub>

---

## 29. Auditory ossicles

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 14.1 Sensory Perception; 7.2 The Skull · id `ossicles` · bone `malleus_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The three tiny bones of the middle ear: the malleus, incus and stapes (auditory ossicles). <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The ossicles are three tiny bones in your ear that pass sound inwards. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 14.1 ¶fs-id2480192]</sub>

*Deeper.* Their Latin names roughly mean hammer, anvil and stirrup. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- They span the space of the middle ear, inside the petrous part of the temporal bone. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>
- The malleus is attached to the eardrum (tympanic membrane). <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>
- The stapes is attached to the inner ear, at the oval window of the cochlea. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 14.1 ¶fs-id2479589]</sub>

*Simpler.* They sit deep in your ear, between the eardrum and the inner ear. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>

*Deeper.* The ear canal leading to the eardrum enters the skull through the external acoustic meatus. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Malleus.** “Hammer”: attached to the eardrum, and joined to the incus. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>
- **Incus.** “Anvil”: between the other two, joined to both. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>
- **Stapes.** “Stirrup”: joined to the incus, and attached to the inner ear at the oval window. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 14.1 ¶fs-id2479589]</sub>
- **A chain of joints.** The three are joined end to end, from the eardrum to the inner ear. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>
- **Footplate of the stapes.** Its flat base fills the oval window. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

*Simpler.* They are a chain of three tiny bones: hammer, anvil and stirrup. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>

*Deeper.* The stapes is the smallest bone in the body. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Sound makes the eardrum vibrate; the ossicles pass the vibrations along to the inner ear. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 14.1 ¶fs-id2480192]</sub>
- Vibrations passing through the oval window set the fluid of the cochlea moving in waves. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2480192]</sub>
- The fluid waves match the frequencies of the sound. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2480192]</sub>

*Simpler.* They pass the eardrum’s vibrations along to the fluid of the inner ear. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 14.1 ¶fs-id2480192]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *membrane* **Tympanic membrane (eardrum).** Vibrates when sound strikes it; the malleus is attached to it. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>
- *organ* **Cochlea.** The inner ear organ of hearing, attached to the stapes through the oval window. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2479589]</sub>
- *opening* **Round window.** A membrane-covered opening that bulges as the fluid in the cochlea moves. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2480192]</sub>
- *bone* **Temporal bone.** Its petrous part contains the middle and inner ear. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>
- *nerve* **Vestibulocochlear nerve.** Carries the signals of hearing from the cochlea to the brainstem. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2479589]</sub>

*Simpler.* They link the eardrum to the cochlea, deep in the temporal bone. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 14.1 ¶fs-id2479589; 7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>

### 6. Development

- They form from cartilage of the embryonic pharyngeal arches. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Abnormal bone growth fixes the stapes in the oval window (otosclerosis).  
   **Damaged:** The stapes.  
   **Patient can’t:** Hear well: sound is no longer passed into the inner ear.  
   **Why:** The stapes must move to send vibrations through the oval window. † <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2480192; † Otosclerosis is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the role of the oval window is.]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A blast, or something pushed into the ear canal.  
   **Damaged:** The eardrum tears.  
   **Patient can’t:** Hear normally on that side.  
   **Why:** The malleus is attached to the eardrum, which must vibrate to move the ossicles. † <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; † A torn eardrum is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the eardrum’s role is.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how sound reaches the inner ear.** <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 14.1 ¶fs-id2480192]</sub>

   - Sound waves travel down the ear canal and strike the eardrum, which vibrates.
   - The malleus, attached to the eardrum, passes the vibration to the incus.
   - The incus passes it to the stapes.
   - The stapes moves the oval window, sending waves through the fluid of the cochlea.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the attachments of the malleus and the stapes.** <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 14.1 ¶fs-id2479589]</sub>

   - The malleus is attached to the eardrum, at the outer end of the chain.
   - The stapes is attached to the inner ear, at the oval window.
   - The incus links them.
   - So vibration passes from the outer to the inner ear.

3. *Predict.* **Predict the effect on hearing if the stapes cannot move, and explain it.** † <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2480192; † Hearing loss from a fixed stapes is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the role of the oval window is.]</sub>

   - Hearing is reduced.
   - The stapes cannot move the oval window.
   - So little vibration reaches the fluid of the cochlea.
   - The sound-sensing cells are hardly stimulated.

### Check yourself

1. Which ossicle is attached to the eardrum?  
   A. Incus · B. Stapes · **C. Malleus** · D. Cochlea  
   *The malleus is attached to the tympanic membrane.* <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243]</sub>

2. Which ossicle is attached to the oval window?  
   **A. Stapes** · B. Malleus · C. Incus · D. All three  
   *The cochlea is attached to the stapes through the oval window.* <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2479589]</sub>

3. Which bone houses the middle ear?  
   A. Sphenoid bone · B. Occipital bone · C. Parietal bone · **D. Temporal bone**  
   *Cavities inside the petrous part of the temporal bone house the middle and inner ears.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>

## 30. Palatine bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 6.4 · id `palatine` · bone `palatine_bone_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**One of a pair of irregular bones that form the back quarter of the hard palate (palatine bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The palatine bones form the back of the bony roof of your mouth. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

*Deeper.* They also form small parts of the side walls of the nasal cavity and of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- Its horizontal plate meets its partner in the midline, at the back of the hard palate. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>
- In front of it are the palatine processes of the maxillae. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253; 7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>
- It is best seen looking at the skull from below. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits at the back of the bony roof of your mouth. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

*Deeper.* The palatine bone also adds a small part to the floor of the orbit, beside the maxilla. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Horizontal plate.** Its largest region; with its partner it forms the back quarter of the hard palate. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>
- **Nasal part.** Adds small areas to the side walls of the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>
- **Orbital part.** A small contribution to the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- **Shape.** An irregular bone, paired with its partner. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

*Simpler.* Its main part is a flat plate at the back of the roof of the mouth. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

*Deeper.* So it reaches from the roof of the mouth up into the nose and the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- With the maxillae, it completes the hard palate, which separates the mouth from the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938; 7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>
- A complete palate keeps the mouth and nose apart, so an infant can suckle. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1547568]</sub>

*Simpler.* It finishes the roof of your mouth, sealing it off from your nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938; 7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bones* **Maxillae.** Their palatine processes form the front three-quarters of the hard palate. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>
- *bone* **Other palatine bone.** Its partner, meeting it in the midline. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>
- *space* **Nasal cavity.** The hard palate forms its floor; the palatine bone adds to its side walls. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253; 7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>
- *space* **Orbit.** A small part of the floor, beside the maxilla. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>

*Simpler.* It joins the maxillae and its partner to make the roof of the mouth. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938; 7.2 ¶fs-id2307253]</sub>

### 6. Development

- During development, its horizontal plate and the maxillae’s palatine processes fuse in the midline, forming the hard palate. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1845434; 7.2 ¶fs-id1547568]</sub>
- Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification). † <sub>[6.4 ¶fs-id1804592; † OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the palatine bone needs an approved source.]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** The two halves of the hard palate fail to fuse during development (cleft palate).  
   **Damaged:** The palatine plates and palatine processes leave a gap in the midline.  
   **Patient can’t:** Suckle well: the infant is at risk of malnutrition until surgery.  
   **Why:** The gap connects the mouth and nasal cavity, so no seal forms. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1547568]</sub>

2. **Injury:** In a severe cleft palate, the gap continues forwards.  
   **Damaged:** The alveolar processes of the maxillae, above the front teeth, also fail to join.  
   **Patient can’t:** Close the upper lip: a cleft lip is also present.  
   **Why:** The lip forms over the joining maxillae at the same time as the palate. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1547568; 7.2 ¶fs-id1845434]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the hard palate forms.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1845434; 7.2 ¶fs-id1547568]</sub>

   - The right and left maxillae come together in the midline.
   - Their palatine processes join.
   - The horizontal plates of the palatine bones join behind them.
   - All four fuse in the midline to form the hard palate.

2. *Compare.* **Compare cleft lip and cleft palate.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1363721; 7.2 ¶fs-id1547568]</sub>

   - Cleft lip: the two sides of the upper lip fail to fuse.
   - It affects about 1 in 1000 births, mostly males.
   - Cleft palate: the two halves of the hard palate fail to fuse; about 1 in 2500 births, more often females.
   - Cleft palate is more severe, as it makes suckling difficult.

3. *Predict.* **Predict why a baby with a cleft palate struggles to feed.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1547568]</sub>

   - The gap connects the mouth with the nasal cavity.
   - So the baby cannot seal the mouth.
   - Without a seal, it cannot suckle well.
   - Surgical repair is needed to prevent malnutrition.

### Check yourself

1. Which part of the hard palate do the palatine bones form?  
   A. The front three-quarters · **B. The back quarter** · C. The whole palate · D. None of it  
   *Their horizontal plates form the posterior quarter of the hard palate.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

2. Which bones join the palatine bones to form the hard palate?  
   **A. The maxillae** · B. The vomer · C. The nasal bones · D. The zygomatic bones  
   *The palatine processes of the maxillae form the rest of the hard palate.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2307253; 7.2 ¶fs-id1965938]</sub>

3. Why does a cleft palate make feeding difficult?  
   A. The tongue is missing · B. The teeth cannot form · C. The jaw cannot open · **D. The mouth and nose are connected, so no seal forms**  
   *The gap connects the oral and nasal cavities, so suckling is very difficult.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1547568]</sub>

## 31. Pancreas

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 23.6 Accessory Organs in Digestion; 17.9 The Endocrine Pancreas · id `pancreas` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A gland behind the stomach that makes digestive juice for the duodenum and hormones that control blood glucose. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509; 17.9 ¶fs-id1907359]</sub>**

*Simpler.* Your pancreas makes juices to digest food and insulin to manage sugar in your blood. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1364084; 17.9 ¶fs-id1977301]</sub>

*Deeper.* It is both exocrine (enzymes into ducts) and endocrine (hormones into the blood). <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It lies across the back of the abdomen, behind the stomach, in the retroperitoneum. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509]</sub>
- Its head sits in the C-shaped curve of the duodenum; its tail reaches the spleen. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509]</sub>

*Simpler.* It lies flat behind your stomach, from the duodenum across to the spleen. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its body extends about 15 cm to the left of the head. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Head, body and tail.** Head in the duodenal curve, body extending left, tail at the hilum of the spleen. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509]</sub>
- **Acini.** Grape-like clusters of cells that make pancreatic juice. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1836151]</sub>
- **Pancreatic islets.** Islands of endocrine cells among the acini (formerly the islets of Langerhans). <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2022250; 17.9 ¶fs-id1907359]</sub>
- **Pancreatic ducts.** The main duct joins the bile duct at the hepatopancreatic ampulla. A smaller accessory duct may open separately. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1836151]</sub>

*Simpler.* Mostly juice-making cells, with small islands of hormone-making cells. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1836151; 23.6 ¶fs-id2022250]</sub>

*Deeper.* In each islet, beta cells (insulin) make up about 75 percent and alpha cells (glucagon) about 20 percent. <sub>[17.9 ¶fs-id1977301]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- It makes over a litre of pancreatic juice a day; its bicarbonate buffers the acidic chyme. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1364084]</sub>
- Protein-digesting enzymes are made inactive, so they cannot digest the pancreas itself. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1895555]</sub>
- Insulin lowers blood glucose; glucagon raises it. <sub>[17.9 ¶tbl-ch18_07]</sub>

*Simpler.* Its juice neutralises stomach acid and digests food; its hormones balance blood sugar. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1364084; 17.9 ¶tbl-ch18_07]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *organ* **Duodenum.** Curves round the head of the pancreas and receives its juice. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2271264; 23.6 ¶fs-id1836151]</sub>
- *duct* **Common bile duct.** Joins the main pancreatic duct just before the duodenum. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1836151]</sub>
- *organ* **Stomach.** Lies in front of the pancreas. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509]</sub>
- *organ* **Spleen.** The tail of the pancreas ends at its hilum. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509]</sub>
- *hormone* **Cholecystokinin.** From I cells of the duodenum; stimulates the release of pancreatic juices and bile. <sub>[23.5 ¶tbl-ch24_07]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is wrapped by the duodenum and shares a duct with the liver. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2271264; 23.6 ¶fs-id1836151]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It develops from buds of the embryonic gut tube that later fuse. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The development of the pancreas.]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- A smaller accessory duct, when present, opens into the duodenum about an inch above the main opening. <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1836151]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Protein-digesting enzymes activated inside the pancreas.  
   **Damaged:** The pancreas itself (pancreatitis).  
   **Patient can’t:** Digest normally without pain, as the gland is inflamed.  
   **Why:** Active enzymes digest the pancreatic tissue. † <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1895555; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The symptoms of pancreatitis.]</sub>

2. **Injury:** An autoimmune attack on the beta cells.  
   **Damaged:** The beta cells of the islets (type 1 diabetes).  
   **Patient can’t:** Make insulin: they need insulin by injection or infusion.  
   **Why:** Without insulin, glucose cannot enter most body cells. <sub>[17.9 ¶fs-id1200718; 17.9 ¶fs-id1489225]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the pancreas makes its protein-digesting enzymes in inactive forms.** <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1895555]</sub>

   - Active enzymes would digest the pancreas.
   - They are released as trypsinogen and other inactive forms.
   - Enteropeptidase in the duodenum activates trypsin.
   - Trypsin then activates the other enzymes.

2. *Compare.* **Compare insulin and glucagon.** <sub>[17.9 ¶fs-id1977301; 17.9 ¶tbl-ch18_07; 17.9 ¶fs-id1751772; 17.9 ¶fs-id1886347]</sub>

   - Insulin comes from beta cells; glucagon from alpha cells.
   - High blood glucose releases insulin; low glucose releases glucagon.
   - Insulin lowers blood glucose; glucagon raises it.
   - Both are controlled by negative feedback.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what happens to the beta cells in long-standing type 2 diabetes.** <sub>[17.9 ¶fs-id1641299]</sub>

   - Body cells become resistant to insulin.
   - The pancreas makes more insulin to compensate.
   - Over time the beta cells become exhausted.
   - Blood glucose then rises further.

### Check yourself

1. Which islet cells make insulin?  
   A. Alpha cells · B. Delta cells · **C. Beta cells** · D. PP cells  
   *Beta cells, about 75 percent of each islet, produce insulin.* <sub>[17.9 ¶fs-id1977301]</sub>

2. What makes pancreatic juice slightly alkaline?  
   **A. Sodium bicarbonate** · B. Hydrochloric acid · C. Bile salts · D. Insulin  
   *Sodium bicarbonate gives pancreatic juice its alkalinity.* <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id1364084]</sub>

3. Where does the head of the pancreas lie?  
   A. At the hilum of the spleen · **B. In the C-shaped curve of the duodenum** · C. Behind the liver · D. In front of the stomach  
   *Its head nestles in the C-shaped curve of the duodenum.* <sub>[23.6 ¶fs-id2094509]</sub>

---

## 32. Parietal bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 7.5; 9.2; 20.5 · id `parietal` · bone `parietal_bone_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**One of a pair of curved bones forming most of the upper sides and top of the skull (parietal bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1975652]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The parietal bones form the top and sides of your head. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1975652]</sub>

*Deeper.* The two parietal bones meet along the top of the skull at the sagittal suture. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It forms most of the upper side of the skull; the right and left meet at the top. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1975652]</sub>
- The frontal bone is in front, the temporal bone below and the occipital bone behind. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1975652]</sub>
- It is one of the flat bones that form the top and sides of the brain case. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1256378]</sub>

*Simpler.* It forms most of the top and side of your head. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1975652]</sub>

*Deeper.* The parietal is one of the four bones that meet at the pterion, the weakest part of the skull. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Sagittal border.** Meets the other parietal bone at the sagittal suture. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- **Frontal border.** Meets the frontal bone at the coronal suture. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- **Occipital border.** Meets the occipital bone at the lambdoid suture. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- **Squamous border.** Meets the squamous part of the temporal bone at the squamous suture. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- **Grooves on the inner surface.** Branches of the artery that supplies the brain’s coverings mark the inner surface of the skull. † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2026215; † OpenStax says the artery’s branches groove the inner surface of the skull; that this includes the parietal bone needs an approved source.]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a curved plate of bone, joined to its neighbours by sutures. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1975652; 7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its sutures twist tightly, interlocking it with its neighbours to strengthen the skull. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1195048]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- As a curved flat bone of the brain case, it encloses and protects the brain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1256378]</sub>
- Its twisting sutures interlock with the neighbouring bones, adding strength. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1195048]</sub>
- Sutures are fibrous joints that allow almost no movement. <sub>[9.2 ¶fs-id2663393]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a strong, curved shield over the brain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1256378]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *suture* **Sagittal suture.** Unites the right and left parietal bones along the midline. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- *suture* **Coronal suture.** Joins it to the frontal bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- *suture* **Lambdoid suture.** Joins it to the occipital bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- *suture* **Squamous suture.** Joins it to the temporal bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>
- *vein* **Superior sagittal sinus.** A large vein of the brain’s coverings, running in the midline beneath the top of the skull. <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id977085]</sub>

*Simpler.* Sutures join it to the frontal, occipital and temporal bones, and to its partner. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification), like the other bones of the top and sides of the brain case. <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1547037]</sub>
- In a baby, wide areas of connective tissue (fontanelles) separate it from its neighbours. <sub>[9.2 ¶fs-id2293668; 7.5 ¶fs-id1547037]</sub>
- The fontanelles narrow greatly during the first year, as the bones enlarge, and become sutures. <sub>[9.2 ¶fs-id2293668]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** The sagittal suture closes too early (craniosynostosis).  
   **Damaged:** The joint between the two parietal bones fuses.  
   **Patient can’t:** Grow across the top of the head: the head becomes long and narrow (scaphocephaly).  
   **Why:** Growth is diverted to the sides of the skull, which grow upwards. <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1031698; 7.5 ¶fs-id2339105]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A blow to the side of the head.  
   **Damaged:** A linear fracture, with lines radiating from the point of impact.  
   **Patient can’t:** Escape the risk of bleeding inside the skull, which can injure the brain.  
   **Why:** A fracture of the brain case can tear the vessels inside it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1972896]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why a newborn’s skull can change shape during birth.** <sub>[9.2 ¶fs-id2293668; 7.5 ¶fs-id1547037]</sub>

   - The brain case bones are still separated by wide fontanelles.
   - These are areas of dense connective tissue.
   - They let the bones push closer together or overlap slightly.
   - So the head can pass through the birth canal.

2. *Compare.* **Compare a suture with a fontanelle.** <sub>[9.2 ¶fs-id2663393; 9.2 ¶fs-id2293668]</sub>

   - Both are fibrous joints between skull bones.
   - A fontanelle is a wide area of connective tissue in a baby’s skull.
   - A suture is a narrow, convoluted fibrous joint in the adult skull.
   - Fontanelles narrow into sutures as the bones grow.

3. *Predict.* **Predict the head shape if the sagittal suture fuses too early, and explain it.** <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1031698]</sub>

   - The skull cannot widen across the top.
   - Growth is diverted to the sides, which grow upwards.
   - The head becomes long, narrow and wedge-shaped (scaphocephaly).
   - The brain still has room to grow normally.

### Check yourself

1. Which suture joins the two parietal bones?  
   A. Coronal · B. Lambdoid · **C. Sagittal** · D. Squamous  
   *The sagittal suture unites the right and left parietal bones.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

2. How do the parietal bones develop?  
   **A. Directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification)** · B. From a cartilage model · C. From the sphenoid · D. From the notochord  
   *The bones of the top and sides of the brain case form directly from membrane.* <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1547037]</sub>

3. What is scaphocephaly?  
   A. A fracture of the parietal bone · B. Bleeding at the pterion · C. An open fontanelle · **D. A long, narrow head from early fusion of the sagittal suture**  
   *Early closure of the sagittal suture diverts growth upwards at the sides.* <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1031698]</sub>

---

## 33. Pisiform

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (and glossary); 8.5 · id `pisiform` · bone `pisiform_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A small pea-shaped bone on the front of the triquetrum, at the base of the hand (pisiform). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: pisiform]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The pisiform is the small round wrist bone at the base of your palm, on the little-finger side. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

*Deeper.* It sits on the front of the triquetrum, not in line with the other bones of its row. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It is the fourth bone of the first row, counting from the thumb side. <sub>[glossary: pisiform; 8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- Unlike the rest of its row, it sits on the front (anterior) surface of the triquetrum. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- From there it projects forwards, forming the bump at the inner base of the hand. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

*Simpler.* Feel the bump at the base of your palm, below the little finger: that is the pisiform. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its only joint is with the triquetrum. <sub>[glossary: pisiform]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Pea shape.** “Pisiform” means pea-shaped. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- **Facet for the triquetrum.** A flat surface on its back, where it meets the triquetrum. <sub>[glossary: pisiform]</sub>
- **Front surface.** Projects forwards, so you can feel it at the base of the hand. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- **Attachment of the flexor retinaculum.** The inner end of the flexor retinaculum anchors to it. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a small round bone that you can feel through the skin. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

*Deeper.* It lies within the tendon of a forearm muscle (flexor carpi ulnaris), like a kneecap within its tendon. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- It anchors the inner end of the flexor retinaculum, which holds the carpal bones in their U shape. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>
- It is the only carpal bone out of line with its row: it sits on the triquetrum. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

*Simpler.* The pisiform anchors the band that roofs the carpal tunnel. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Triquetrum.** Its only joint: it sits on the triquetrum’s front surface. <sub>[glossary: pisiform; 8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- *ligament* **Flexor retinaculum.** Attached on the little-finger side to the hamate and pisiform. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>
- *space* **Carpal tunnel.** The flexor retinaculum it anchors forms the roof of the tunnel. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>
- *muscle* **Flexor carpi ulnaris.** Its tendon attaches to the pisiform. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>
- *nerve and artery* **Ulnar nerve and artery.** Pass beside it into the hand, outside the carpal tunnel. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits on the triquetrum and anchors the roof of the carpal tunnel. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; 8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification), like all limb bones except the clavicle. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Its single ossification centre appears last of the carpal bones, at about the twelfth year. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives about the twelfth year (supporting only).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A fall onto the heel of the hand on the little-finger side.  
   **Damaged:** The pisiform breaks.  
   **Patient can’t:** Bend the wrist towards the little-finger side without pain.  
   **Why:** A forearm muscle tendon attaches to it and pulls on the broken bone. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Long pressure on the base of the palm, as in long-distance cycling.  
   **Damaged:** The ulnar nerve beside the pisiform.  
   **Patient can’t:** Spread and close the fingers strongly; the little finger may feel numb.  
   **Why:** The nerve passes beside the pisiform and the hook of the hamate into the hand. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why you can feel the pisiform when you cannot feel most carpal bones.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

   - It sits on the front of the triquetrum, not in line with its row.
   - It projects forwards.
   - So it forms a bump at the inner base of the hand.
   - Most carpal bones lie deeper, in their two rows.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the pisiform and the hook of the hamate.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: hook of the hamate bone; 8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

   - Both are on the little-finger side of the hand.
   - The pisiform is a whole bone sitting on the triquetrum.
   - The hook is a bony extension on the front of the hamate.
   - The flexor retinaculum attaches on this side to the hamate and pisiform.

3. *Predict.* **After a long ride, a cyclist’s little finger feels numb. Predict the nerve affected and where.** † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

   - The ulnar nerve.
   - It is pressed where it passes beside the pisiform into the hand.
   - The pressure comes from leaning on the handlebars.
   - The median nerve, in the carpal tunnel, is spared.

### Check yourself

1. What does “pisiform” mean?  
   A. Hook-shaped · B. Moon-shaped · **C. Pea-shaped** · D. Three-cornered  
   *“Pisiform” means pea-shaped.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

2. Which bone does the pisiform sit on?  
   **A. Triquetrum** · B. Hamate · C. Lunate · D. Ulna  
   *It sits on the front surface of the triquetrum.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

3. What attaches to the pisiform?  
   A. The annular ligament · B. The interosseous membrane · C. The coracoclavicular ligament · **D. The flexor retinaculum**  
   *The flexor retinaculum attaches medially to the hamate and pisiform.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

## 34. Quadriceps femoris

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs; 11.1; 13.4; 14.3; 16.4 · id `quadriceps-femoris` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The group of four muscles at the front of the thigh that extend and stabilise the knee. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The quadriceps are the big muscles on the front of your thigh. They straighten your knee. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>

*Deeper.* All four join one tendon to the patella, which continues as the patellar ligament to the tibial tuberosity. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- They fill the anterior compartment of the thigh. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>
- Rectus femoris lies in front, vastus lateralis on the outer side and vastus medialis on the inner side. Vastus intermedius lies between them, deep. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>

*Simpler.* They cover the front and sides of your thigh, above the kneecap. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>

*Deeper.* Only the rectus femoris arises from the hip bone; the three vasti arise from the femur. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Rectus femoris.** Arises from the anterior inferior iliac spine and above the acetabulum. It also helps raise the knee. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>
- **Vastus lateralis.** Arises from the greater trochanter, the intertrochanteric line and the linea aspera. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>
- **Vastus medialis.** Arises from the linea aspera and the intertrochanteric line. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>
- **Vastus intermedius.** Arises from the upper shaft of the femur, deep to the rectus femoris. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03; 11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>
- **Quadriceps tendon.** The tendon common to all four. It inserts into the patella. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>

*Simpler.* Four muscles, one shared tendon, one kneecap. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>

*Deeper.* Their motor units are large, up to about 1,000 fibres each, suited to force rather than fine control. <sub>[14.3 ¶fs-id2763982]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Pulling through the patella and patellar ligament, they extend the leg at the knee, as in kicking. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754; 11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>
- The rectus femoris crosses the hip too, so it also flexes the thigh. <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03; 11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>
- The hamstrings, behind, are their antagonists. <sub>[11.1 ¶tbl-ch11_01]</sub>

*Simpler.* They straighten the knee; the front one also lifts the thigh. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754; 11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Patella.** Lies in the quadriceps tendon. The tendon continues below it as the patellar ligament. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>
- *bone* **Tibial tuberosity.** Where the patellar ligament attaches. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>
- *muscle* **Hamstrings.** In the posterior compartment. They flex the knee, opposing the quadriceps. <sub>[11.1 ¶tbl-ch11_01; 11.6 ¶fs-id2012638]</sub>
- *muscle* **Sartorius.** A band-like muscle crossing the front of the thigh, also in the anterior compartment. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>
- *nerve* **Femoral nerve.** A major nerve of the lumbar plexus, to the front of the thigh. † <sub>[13.4 ¶fs-id1472046; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the femoral nerve supplies the quadriceps.]</sub>

*Simpler.* They pull on the kneecap and are opposed by the hamstrings. <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754; 11.1 ¶tbl-ch11_01]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Limb muscles develop from general mesoderm. Satellite cells later repair the fibres. <sub>[10.9 ¶fs-id1959103; 10.9 ¶fs-id2329008]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- Without use, they waste quickly (disuse atrophy), for example after a leg fracture. <sub>[10.9 ¶fs-id2269378]</sub>
- Age-related loss (sarcopenia) is slowed, though not stopped, by exercise. <sub>[10.6 ¶fs-id2267886; 10.6 ¶fs-id1425445]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Tapping the patellar ligament below the kneecap.  
   **Damaged:** The knee-jerk (stretch) reflex of the quadriceps.  
   **Patient can’t:** Show a normal knee-jerk reflex if the reflex arc is damaged.  
   **Why:** Stretching the muscle activates its spindles, which excite its motor neurons directly. <sub>[16.4 ¶fs-id1956663]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A leg kept in plaster for weeks.  
   **Damaged:** The quadriceps, through disuse.  
   **Patient can’t:** Straighten the knee strongly when the plaster comes off.  
   **Why:** Unused muscle loses structural proteins and shrinks. <sub>[10.9 ¶fs-id2269378; 10.9 ¶fs-id1645648]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the quadriceps extend the knee though none of them attaches to the tibia directly.** <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>

   - All four join the quadriceps tendon.
   - The tendon inserts into the patella.
   - Below the patella it continues as the patellar ligament.
   - The ligament attaches to the tibial tuberosity, so pulling it straightens the knee.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the rectus femoris and the vastus lateralis.** <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03; 11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>

   - Both extend the knee through the quadriceps tendon.
   - The rectus femoris arises from the hip bone, so it also flexes the thigh.
   - The vastus lateralis arises from the femur and acts on the knee only.
   - The rectus femoris lies in front; the vastus lateralis on the outer side.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what a knee-jerk test shows if the reflex arc is intact.** <sub>[16.4 ¶fs-id1956663]</sub>

   - The tap stretches the quadriceps.
   - Muscle spindles signal into the spinal cord.
   - Their fibres excite the motor neuron directly.
   - The quadriceps contract and the leg kicks forwards.

### Check yourself

1. Which quadriceps muscle also flexes the thigh at the hip?  
   A. Vastus lateralis · B. Vastus medialis · C. Vastus intermedius · **D. Rectus femoris**  
   *The rectus femoris arises from the hip bone, so it also helps raise the knee (flex the thigh).* <sub>[11.6 ¶fig-ch11_06_03]</sub>

2. Where does the patellar ligament attach?  
   **A. Tibial tuberosity** · B. Head of the fibula · C. Medial malleolus · D. Linea aspera  
   *The patellar ligament attaches to the tibial tuberosity.* <sub>[11.6 ¶fs-id2591754]</sub>

3. Which group opposes the quadriceps at the knee?  
   A. Adductors · **B. Hamstrings** · C. Gluteal muscles · D. Fibular muscles  
   *The hamstrings flex the leg, whereas the quadriceps extend it.* <sub>[11.1 ¶tbl-ch11_01]</sub>
