## 35. Radius

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb; 8.5; 9.4; 9.5; 9.6; 20.5 · id `radius` · bone `radius_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The lateral bone of the forearm, on the thumb side (radius). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842; 8.2 ¶fs-id1415448]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The radius is the forearm bone on the thumb side. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>

*Deeper.* It turns around the ulna as the palm turns backwards and forwards (pronation and supination). <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1837207]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It runs from the elbow to the wrist, alongside the ulna. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842; 8.2 ¶fs-id1415448]</sub>
- In the anatomical position it is lateral, on the thumb side; the ulna is medial. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1415448; 8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- Its head meets the humerus and ulna at the elbow. Its lower end meets the carpal bones at the wrist. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>

*Simpler.* It runs from your elbow to your wrist on the thumb side. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>

*Deeper.* When the palm faces backwards, the radius crosses over the ulna and the two form an X. <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1547390]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Head.** A disc at the top. Its hollow meets the capitulum of the humerus; its rim turns against the ulna. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- **Neck.** The narrow part just below the head. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- **Radial tuberosity.** An oval bump below the neck, on the inner side, where a muscle attaches. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- **Interosseous border.** A ridge on the inner side of the shaft. The interosseous membrane attaches along it. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- **Ulnar notch.** A shallow hollow at the lower end that meets the head of the ulna. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- **Styloid process.** A pointed projection at the lower outer end. Ligaments of the wrist joint attach to it. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- **Carpal surface.** The smooth lower end. It meets the scaphoid and lunate at the wrist joint. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842; 8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

*Simpler.* It has a round head at the elbow and a wide end at the wrist. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its styloid process reaches further down than the ulna’s. This limits how far the hand bends to the thumb side. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Its disc-shaped head turns within a ring of ligament at a pivot joint. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1636208; 9.6 ¶fs-id2602265]</sub>
- Its shaft is slightly curved, so as the head turns, its lower end swings across the ulna. <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1837207]</sub>
- The broad lower end meets two carpal bones directly, so force from a fall on the hand passes into it. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937; 8.2 ¶fs-id1989621]</sub>

*Simpler.* Its round head spins. This lets your forearm twist and your palm turn over. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1636208; 9.5 ¶fs-id1837207]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joint* **Elbow joint.** Its head meets the capitulum (humeroradial joint), inside the same capsule as the elbow hinge. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id1355969; 8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- *joint* **Proximal radioulnar joint.** A pivot joint: the head turns in the radial notch of the ulna. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1636208; 9.5 ¶fs-id1401596]</sub>
- *joint* **Distal radioulnar joint.** The ulnar notch meets the head of the ulna. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- *joint* **Wrist (radiocarpal) joint.** A condyloid joint: the lower end meets the scaphoid and lunate. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1434553; 8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>
- *ligament* **Annular ligament.** Encircles the head and holds it against the ulna as it turns. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2602265]</sub>
- *membrane* **Interosseous membrane.** A sheet of dense connective tissue joining the shafts of the radius and ulna. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1266017; 8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- *artery* **Radial artery.** Runs down the forearm alongside the radius to the wrist. <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id1595346]</sub>

*Simpler.* It joins the humerus, the ulna and the wrist bones, and the radial artery runs beside it. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842; 20.5 ¶fs-id1595346]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification). <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Bone first appears in the shaft by about the twelfth week. Centres in the ends appear later, usually after birth. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A fall onto the outstretched hand, often in an older person with osteoporosis.  
   **Damaged:** The lower end of the radius breaks across, and the lower fragment is pushed backwards and upwards (Colles fracture).  
   **Patient can’t:** Use the wrist normally: the forearm has a “dinner fork” bend just above it.  
   **Why:** The hand is carried backwards with the displaced fragment. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2142217]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A young child is lifted or swung by the hand.  
   **Damaged:** The head of the radius slips partly out of the annular ligament (“pulled elbow”).  
   **Patient can’t:** Turn the palm up, and the child holds the arm still.  
   **Why:** The annular ligament normally holds the head in place as it turns. † <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2602265; † “Pulled elbow” (radial head subluxation) in young children is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the radius and ulna move as the forearm turns from palm forwards to palm backwards.** <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1837207; 9.5 ¶fs-id1547390]</sub>

   - This movement is pronation.
   - The head of the radius turns at the proximal radioulnar joint, a pivot joint.
   - The shaft is slightly curved, so the lower end of the radius crosses over the ulna.
   - The two bones end in an X shape; supination returns them to parallel.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the styloid processes of the radius and the ulna.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842; 8.2 ¶fs-id1266017]</sub>

   - Radius: a pointed projection at its lower outer end.
   - Ulna: a short projection from the back of its head.
   - The radial styloid reaches further down.
   - So the hand bends less far to the thumb side than to the little-finger side.

3. *Predict.* **A 70-year-old woman falls on her outstretched hand. Predict the fracture and how the wrist looks.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2142217]</sub>

   - A Colles fracture: the lower end of the radius breaks across.
   - The lower fragment is pushed backwards and upwards.
   - The forearm has a “dinner fork” bend just above the wrist.
   - It is the most common forearm fracture, especially with osteoporosis.

### Check yourself

1. Which part of the radius meets the capitulum of the humerus?  
   A. Ulnar notch · **B. Head** · C. Styloid process · D. Radial tuberosity  
   *The small hollow on the top of the head meets the capitulum at the elbow.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>

2. At which joint does the head of the radius turn during pronation?  
   **A. Proximal radioulnar joint** · B. Wrist (radiocarpal) joint · C. Midcarpal joint · D. Humeroulnar joint  
   *The proximal radioulnar joint is a pivot joint: the head turns in the radial notch of the ulna.* <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1636208; 9.5 ¶fs-id1837207]</sub>

3. What causes the “dinner fork” shape after a Colles fracture?  
   A. The ulna dislocates forwards · B. The scaphoid breaks · C. The wrist swells · **D. The lower fragment of the radius is pushed backwards**  
   *The distal fragment is driven backwards and upwards, carrying the hand with it.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2142217]</sub>

---

## 36. Rotator cuff

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs; 9.6 Anatomy of Selected Synovial Joints · id `rotator-cuff` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**Four short muscles from the scapula whose tendons ring the shoulder joint and hold the humeral head in its socket. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2421860; 9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The rotator cuff is a ring of four muscles that keeps your shoulder joint together. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

*Deeper.* They act as “dynamic ligaments”, adjusting their pull to give the shoulder joint its main support. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- All four arise from the scapula and attach to the greater or lesser tubercle of the humerus. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>
- Their tendons encircle the head of the humerus and fuse with the joint capsule in front, above and behind. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

*Simpler.* They run from your shoulder blade to the top of your arm bone, wrapping round the joint. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

*Deeper.* The fused tendons thicken the capsule. That thickening is what is called the rotator cuff. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Supraspinatus.** Above the spine of the scapula, from the supraspinous fossa. It abducts the arm. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482; 11.5 ¶fig-ch11_05_03]</sub>
- **Infraspinatus.** Below the spine of the scapula, from the infraspinous fossa. It laterally rotates the arm. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482; 11.5 ¶fig-ch11_05_03]</sub>
- **Teres minor.** From the lateral border of the scapula. It laterally rotates and extends the arm. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482; 11.5 ¶fig-ch11_05_03]</sub>
- **Subscapularis.** From the front of the scapula (subscapular fossa). It medially rotates the arm. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482; 11.5 ¶fig-ch11_05_03]</sub>

*Simpler.* Three are on the back of the shoulder blade and one is on its front. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482; 11.5 ¶fig-ch11_05_03]</sub>

*Deeper.* A memory aid for the four is SITS: supraspinatus, infraspinatus, teres minor, subscapularis. † <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2421860; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The memory aid SITS.]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- The shoulder has the largest range of motion of any joint, at the cost of stability. Muscles give its main support. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2305343; 9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>
- Constant small adjustments in their pull keep the humeral head centred in the glenoid cavity. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>
- Two bursae, subacromial and subscapular, stop the tendons rubbing on the scapula. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

*Simpler.* They are like living ligaments: they tighten as needed to keep the joint steady. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *muscle* **Deltoid.** Lies over the cuff, capping the shoulder. It is the main abductor of the arm. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482]</sub>
- *bursa* **Subacromial bursa.** Sits between the cuff tendons and the acromion, reducing friction. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>
- *bone* **Greater and lesser tubercles.** Bony attachment sites on the humerus for muscles acting across the shoulder. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2110691; 9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>
- *muscle* **Teres major.** Lies below the teres minor but is not part of the cuff. It extends and medially rotates the arm. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482]</sub>
- *joint* **Glenohumeral joint.** The joint the cuff supports. Its glenoid cavity is about a third the size of the humeral head. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2305343; 9.6 ¶fs-id1231336]</sub>

*Simpler.* The cuff lies under the deltoid and is protected from the acromion by a bursa. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482; 9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Limb muscles, including these, develop from general mesoderm. Many myoblasts fuse to make each fibre. <sub>[10.9 ¶fs-id1959103]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- Repeated use of the arm in abduction, as in throwing, swimming or racquet sports, can wear and tear the cuff. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id1894060]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Years of throwing fast pitches overhead.  
   **Damaged:** The rotator cuff tendons, which become pinched and inflamed and may tear from the bone.  
   **Patient can’t:** Raise the arm overhead without pain, or with full strength.  
   **Why:** Repeated overhead movement squeezes the tendons beneath the acromion. <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2421860; 9.6 ¶fs-id1894060]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A fall onto the hand with the arm fully abducted.  
   **Damaged:** The shoulder joint, which dislocates downwards (inferiorly).  
   **Patient can’t:** Move the arm at the shoulder.  
   **Why:** The cuff and ligaments support the joint in front, above and behind, but not below. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id1894060]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why most shoulder dislocations are inferior.** <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id1894060; 9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

   - The humeral head is supported in front, above and behind.
   - This support comes from the rotator cuff and ligaments.
   - Below, the joint has little support.
   - So a force on the fully abducted arm drives the head downwards.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the actions of the infraspinatus and the subscapularis.** <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482; 11.1 ¶fs-id2260527; 9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

   - Both are rotator cuff muscles from the scapula.
   - The infraspinatus lies behind and laterally rotates the arm.
   - The subscapularis lies in front and medially rotates it.
   - So they are antagonists for rotation, while both steady the joint.

3. *Predict.* **Predict which movements hurt in a baseball pitcher with an inflamed rotator cuff.** <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2421860; 9.6 ¶fs-id1894060]</sub>

   - Bringing the arm overhead to throw.
   - The tendons are pinched in this position.
   - Repeated movement inflames them further.
   - A tendon may eventually tear away from the bone.

### Check yourself

1. Which muscle is not part of the rotator cuff?  
   A. Supraspinatus · B. Teres minor · **C. Teres major** · D. Subscapularis  
   *The cuff is the supraspinatus, infraspinatus, teres minor and subscapularis. The teres major is not part of it.* <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2421860]</sub>

2. Which rotator cuff muscle medially rotates the arm?  
   A. Infraspinatus · **B. Subscapularis** · C. Teres minor · D. Supraspinatus  
   *The subscapularis, on the front of the scapula, medially rotates the arm.* <sub>[11.5 ¶fs-id2501482]</sub>

3. What gives the shoulder joint its main support?  
   A. Its deep bony socket · B. The joint capsule alone · **C. The rotator cuff muscles** · D. The biceps brachii  
   *The rotator cuff muscles act as dynamic ligaments and give the main support.* <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2429784]</sub>

---

## 37. Small intestine

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 23.5 The Small and Large Intestines · id `small-intestine` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The longest part of the digestive tract, where most digestion and practically all absorption take place. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2142144]</sub>**

*Simpler.* Your small intestine is a long, coiled tube that takes the goodness from your food. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2142144]</sub>

*Deeper.* It is “small” only in diameter: about 2.5 cm, against 7.6 cm for the large intestine. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2142144]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It runs from the pyloric sphincter of the stomach to the caecum of the large intestine. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2271264; 23.5 ¶fs-id1921294]</sub>
- The jejunum and ileum hang from the posterior abdominal wall by the mesentery, framed by the large intestine. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1921294]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is coiled up in the middle of your abdomen, ringed by the large intestine. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1921294]</sub>

*Deeper.* The duodenum curves behind the peritoneum (retroperitoneal) round the head of the pancreas. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2271264]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Duodenum.** The shortest part, about 25 cm. It receives bile and pancreatic juice. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2271264; 23.5 ¶fs-id2227124]</sub>
- **Jejunum.** About 0.9 m in life. Its name means “empty”. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2311182]</sub>
- **Ileum.** The longest part, about 1.8 m. It joins the caecum at the ileocaecal valve. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1921294]</sub>
- **Circular folds.** Deep ridges of mucosa and submucosa that make chyme spiral and slow down. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1377788]</sub>
- **Villi and microvilli.** Finger-like projections and their tiny surface extensions, the brush border. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1272744; 23.5 ¶fs-id810619]</sub>

*Simpler.* Three parts: duodenum, jejunum and ileum, lined with millions of tiny folds and fingers. † <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2142277; 23.5 ¶fs-id1272744; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The number of villi (“millions”).]</sub>

*Deeper.* Circular folds, villi and microvilli increase its absorptive surface more than 600-fold. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2020227]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Each villus holds a capillary bed and a lacteal, so absorbed nutrients pass straight into blood or lymph. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1272744]</sub>
- Enzymes fixed to the microvilli finish digesting carbohydrates and proteins. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id810619]</sub>
- Segmentation mixes chyme with digestive juices and presses it against the mucosa. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1837941]</sub>

*Simpler.* A huge, finger-covered surface and churning movements help it absorb food. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2020227; 23.5 ¶fs-id1837941]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *organ* **Stomach.** Releases chyme into the duodenum through the pyloric sphincter. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2271264]</sub>
- *duct* **Bile duct and pancreatic duct.** Join at the hepatopancreatic ampulla and open into the duodenum. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2227124]</sub>
- *organ* **Large intestine (caecum).** Receives food residue from the ileum through the ileocaecal valve. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1921294; 23.5 ¶fs-id1764904]</sub>
- *artery* **Superior mesenteric artery.** Its main arterial supply. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2240902]</sub>
- *vein* **Hepatic portal vein.** Carries nutrient-rich blood from it to the liver. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2240902]</sub>

*Simpler.* It starts at the stomach and ends at the large intestine, and its blood goes to the liver. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2271264; 23.5 ¶fs-id1921294; 23.5 ¶fs-id2240902]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It develops from the midgut of the embryo. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The development of the small intestine.]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- Peyer’s patches in the ileum are most prominent in young people and fade with age. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1482787]</sub>
- Some human populations keep making lactase as adults; in others, lactose intolerance is common. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1632981]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Too little lactase made by the absorptive cells.  
   **Damaged:** Digestion of lactose, the milk sugar (lactose intolerance).  
   **Patient can’t:** Eat dairy without gas, cramps, bloating and diarrhoea.  
   **Why:** Bacteria in the large intestine ferment the undigested lactose, making gas. <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1632981; 23.5 ¶fs-id1485386]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Damage that flattens the villi.  
   **Damaged:** The absorptive surface of the small intestine.  
   **Patient can’t:** Absorb nutrients normally.  
   **Why:** The surface area for absorption falls sharply. † <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2020227; 23.5 ¶fs-id1272744; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Conditions that flatten the villi and their effects.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the small intestine has such a large absorptive surface.** <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1377788; 23.5 ¶fs-id1272744; 23.5 ¶fs-id2020227]</sub>

   - Circular folds make chyme spiral and slow down.
   - Villi cover the folds, about 20–40 per square millimetre.
   - Microvilli cover the cells of each villus.
   - Together they increase the surface more than 600-fold.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the duodenum and the ileum.** <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2271264; 23.5 ¶fs-id2041977; 23.5 ¶fs-id1921294; 23.5 ¶fs-id1482787]</sub>

   - Duodenum: shortest, about 25 cm, partly retroperitoneal.
   - It receives bile and pancreatic juice and has Brunner’s glands.
   - Ileum: longest, about 1.8 m, on the mesentery.
   - It has Peyer’s patches and ends at the ileocaecal valve.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what happens in the colon of someone with lactose intolerance after drinking milk.** <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1485386; 23.5 ¶fs-id1761464]</sub>

   - Lactose passes undigested through the small intestine.
   - Bacteria in the large intestine ferment it.
   - This produces gas, including hydrogen.
   - Cramps, bloating and diarrhoea follow.

### Check yourself

1. Which is the longest part of the small intestine?  
   A. Duodenum · B. Jejunum · **C. Ileum** · D. Caecum  
   *The ileum, about 1.8 m long, is the longest part.* <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1921294]</sub>

2. What is a lacteal?  
   **A. A lymphatic capillary in a villus** · B. A gland in the duodenum · C. A fold of the mucosa · D. An enzyme for lactose  
   *Each villus contains a lymphatic capillary called a lacteal.* <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id1272744]</sub>

3. Where do bile and pancreatic juice enter the small intestine?  
   A. Ileum · **B. Duodenum** · C. Jejunum · D. Ileocaecal valve  
   *Both reach the duodenum through the hepatopancreatic ampulla.* <sub>[23.5 ¶fs-id2227124]</sub>

---

## 38. Sternocleidomastoid

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 11.2 Naming Skeletal Muscles; 11.3 Axial Muscles of the Head, Neck, and Back; 13.4 · id `sternocleidomastoid` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The neck muscle from the sternum and clavicle to the mastoid process that turns and tilts the head. <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191; 11.3 ¶fs-id2455581]</sub>**

*Simpler.* It is the long muscle on each side of your neck that turns your head. <sub>[11.3 ¶fs-id2455581]</sub>

*Deeper.* Acting alone it turns the head to the opposite side; both together flex the head. <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It runs up the side of the neck, from the sternum and clavicle to the skull behind the ear. <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191; 11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05]</sub>
- It inserts on the mastoid process of the temporal bone and on the occipital bone. <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05]</sub>

*Simpler.* You can feel it on the side of your neck when you turn your head. <sub>[11.3 ¶fs-id2455581]</sub>

*Deeper.* Seen from the side, it divides the neck into anterior and posterior triangles. <sub>[11.3 ¶fs-id2455581]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Sternal head.** Arises from the sternum (“sterno” in the name). <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191]</sub>
- **Clavicular head.** Arises from the clavicle (“cleido” in the name). <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191]</sub>
- **Insertion.** On the mastoid process of the temporal bone (“mastoid” in the name), and the occipital bone. <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191; 11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05]</sub>
- **Name.** Named for its attachments, with the origin first: sterno, cleido, mastoid. <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191]</sub>

*Simpler.* Its long name lists where it starts and ends: breastbone, collarbone, mastoid. <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191]</sub>

*Deeper.* When a muscle is named for its attachments, the origin is always named first. <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- One muscle alone turns the head to the opposite side and tilts it to its own side. <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05; 11.3 ¶fs-id2455581]</sub>
- Both together flex the head forwards. <sub>[11.3 ¶fs-id2455581; 11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05]</sub>

*Simpler.* One side turns your head; both sides bend it forwards. <sub>[11.3 ¶fs-id2455581; 11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Temporal bone (mastoid process).** Its insertion, behind the ear. <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191]</sub>
- *muscle* **Splenius capitis.** Tilts and turns the head to its own side; both together extend the head. <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05]</sub>
- *muscle* **Semispinalis capitis.** Extends the head when both sides act, opposing the sternocleidomastoids. <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05]</sub>
- *nerve* **Accessory nerve (cranial nerve XI).** A purely motor cranial nerve to muscles of the neck. <sub>[13.4 ¶tbl-ch13_03; 13.4 ¶fs-id1830197]</sub>
- *region* **Triangles of the neck.** Its line separates the anterior and posterior triangles. <sub>[11.3 ¶fs-id2455581]</sub>

*Simpler.* It works with the deep neck muscles to move the head, and its nerve is a cranial nerve. <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05; 13.4 ¶tbl-ch13_03]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Skeletal muscle of the head develops from general mesoderm; trunk muscles come from somites. † <sub>[10.9 ¶fs-id1959103; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Which embryonic tissue the sternocleidomastoid itself comes from.]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- Its two heads, sternal and clavicular, can be seen and felt separately low in the neck. † <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the two heads are visible separately.]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A baby born with a shortened sternocleidomastoid on one side.  
   **Damaged:** The sternocleidomastoid (congenital torticollis, wry neck).  
   **Patient can’t:** Hold the head straight: it tilts to the short side and turns away.  
   **Why:** The short muscle pulls constantly, tilting to its own side and turning to the opposite. † <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Congenital torticollis.]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Injury to the accessory nerve in the neck.  
   **Damaged:** The nerve to the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius.  
   **Patient can’t:** Turn the head strongly to the opposite side, or shrug that shoulder.  
   **Why:** Both muscles lose their motor supply from cranial nerve XI. † <sub>[13.4 ¶tbl-ch13_03; 11.5 ¶tbl-ch11_08; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The accessory nerve’s supply to the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius, and the signs of its injury.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the left sternocleidomastoid turns the face to the right.** <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05; 11.2 ¶fs-id2279191]</sub>

   - It runs from the front (sternum, clavicle) to the back of the skull (mastoid).
   - Pulling the mastoid down and forwards rotates the head.
   - The face turns to the opposite side.
   - So the left muscle turns the face to the right.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the actions of the sternocleidomastoids and the semispinalis capitis acting on both sides.** <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05; 11.1 ¶fs-id2260527]</sub>

   - Both sternocleidomastoids together flex the head.
   - Both semispinalis capitis muscles together extend it.
   - So the two pairs are antagonists.
   - Acting on one side, each pair turns and tilts the head.

3. *Predict.* **Predict the head position in a baby whose right sternocleidomastoid is short.** † <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Congenital torticollis.]</sub>

   - The short muscle pulls constantly.
   - The head tilts to the right.
   - The face turns to the left.
   - This is congenital torticollis.

### Check yourself

1. Where does the sternocleidomastoid insert?  
   A. Sternum · B. Clavicle · **C. Mastoid process of the temporal bone** · D. Spinous process of C7  
   *It inserts on the mastoid process of the temporal bone, named last in its name.* <sub>[11.2 ¶fs-id2279191]</sub>

2. What does one sternocleidomastoid do on its own?  
   A. Extends the head · **B. Rotates the head to the opposite side** · C. Elevates the scapula · D. Depresses the mandible  
   *Individually it rotates the head to the opposite side.* <sub>[11.3 ¶tbl-ch11_05]</sub>

3. Which neck regions does it separate?  
   **A. Anterior and posterior triangles** · B. Thorax and abdomen · C. Pharynx and larynx · D. Left and right carotid sheaths  
   *Seen from the side, it divides the neck into anterior and posterior triangles.* <sub>[11.3 ¶fs-id2455581]</sub>

---

## 39. Stomach

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 23.4 The Stomach; 23.3 · id `stomach` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The J-shaped bag between the oesophagus and the duodenum that holds food, churns it and starts digesting protein. † <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2151539; 23.4 ¶fs-id1899671; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The stomach’s J shape.]</sub>**

*Simpler.* Your stomach stores a meal and mixes it with acid juices before passing it on. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1477027; 23.4 ¶fs-id1890948]</sub>

*Deeper.* It turns food into chyme and releases it into the small intestine a little at a time. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1477027]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It lies just below the oesophagus and links it to the duodenum. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2151539]</sub>
- Its two ends are fixed; the part between them can move and change size. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2151539]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits in the upper abdomen, under the diaphragm, mostly on the left. † <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the stomach lies mostly on the left.]</sub>

*Deeper.* The lesser omentum ties it to the liver; the greater omentum hangs from its greater curvature. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1386858]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Cardia.** Where the oesophagus joins and food enters. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>
- **Fundus.** The dome above and to the left of the cardia, under the diaphragm. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>
- **Body.** The main part of the stomach. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>
- **Pylorus.** The funnel to the duodenum, ending at the pyloric sphincter, which controls emptying. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>
- **Rugae.** Large folds of mucosa and submucosa that form when the stomach is empty. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>

*Simpler.* Four regions, from top to bottom: cardia, fundus, body and pylorus. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>

*Deeper.* It has an extra, inner oblique layer of smooth muscle, so it can churn food vigorously. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1990056]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Empty it is about the size of a fist, yet it can stretch to hold about 4 litres. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2151539]</sub>
- Parietal cells make hydrochloric acid and intrinsic factor; chief cells make pepsinogen. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1959017; 23.4 ¶fs-id1905668]</sub>
- Mucus rich in bicarbonate, tight junctions and fast cell renewal protect the stomach from digesting itself. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1896856]</sub>

*Simpler.* It stretches to hold a meal, makes strong acid, and protects itself with a mucus lining. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2151539; 23.4 ¶fs-id1959017; 23.4 ¶fs-id1896856]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *organ* **Oesophagus.** Enters through the oesophageal hiatus of the diaphragm and joins at the cardia. <sub>[23.3 ¶fs-id1984077; 23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>
- *organ* **Duodenum.** Receives chyme through the pyloric sphincter. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>
- *organ* **Liver.** Joined to the lesser curvature by the lesser omentum. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1386858]</sub>
- *membrane* **Greater omentum.** Runs from the greater curvature to the posterior abdominal wall. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1386858]</sub>
- *hormone* **Gastrin.** Made mainly by G cells of the pyloric antrum. It increases gastric secretion and emptying. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1388512; 23.4 ¶tbl-ch24_06]</sub>

*Simpler.* Food comes in from the oesophagus and goes on to the duodenum. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It develops as a widening of the foregut, the same tube that also gives rise to the lung bud. † <sub>[22.7 ¶fs-id2485319; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The development of the stomach from the foregut.]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- Its size and position change with every meal, as it fills, churns and empties. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2151539]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Long use of non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) such as aspirin, or Helicobacter pylori infection.  
   **Damaged:** The mucosal barrier, letting acid erode the lining (a peptic ulcer).  
   **Patient can’t:** Eat without “burning” pain and indigestion.  
   **Why:** Gastric juice eats deeper into the unprotected mucosa. <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2023452; 23.4 ¶fs-id1632870; 23.4 ¶fs-id1070208]</sub>

2. **Injury:** An ulcer that erodes right through the stomach wall.  
   **Damaged:** The whole wall, now perforated, and the peritoneum.  
   **Patient can’t:** Recover without surgery.  
   **Why:** Stomach contents leak through the hole and inflame the peritoneum (peritonitis). <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2071941]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the stomach does not digest itself.** <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1896856]</sub>

   - A thick mucus coat rich in bicarbonate neutralises acid.
   - Tight junctions stop gastric juice getting between the cells.
   - Stem cells replace damaged surface cells quickly.
   - Together these form the mucosal barrier.

2. *Compare.* **Compare parietal cells and chief cells.** <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1959017; 23.4 ¶fs-id1905668]</sub>

   - Both lie in the gastric glands.
   - Parietal cells make hydrochloric acid and intrinsic factor.
   - Chief cells make pepsinogen.
   - The acid converts pepsinogen into active pepsin.

3. *Predict.* **Predict the effect of the enterogastric reflex when the duodenum fills with chyme.** <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1352023]</sub>

   - Distension of the duodenum triggers the reflex.
   - Gastric secretion is inhibited.
   - The pyloric sphincter closes.
   - So no more chyme enters until the duodenum is ready.

### Check yourself

1. Which cells secrete hydrochloric acid?  
   A. Chief cells · **B. Parietal cells** · C. G cells · D. Mucous neck cells  
   *Parietal cells produce hydrochloric acid and intrinsic factor.* <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id1959017]</sub>

2. Which region of the stomach connects to the duodenum?  
   **A. Pylorus** · B. Fundus · C. Cardia · D. Body  
   *The funnel-shaped pylorus connects the stomach to the duodenum.* <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2052808]</sub>

3. What causes most peptic ulcers?  
   A. Too much mucus · B. Gastrin deficiency · C. Lack of intrinsic factor · **D. NSAIDs or Helicobacter pylori infection**  
   *The majority of ulcers are caused by NSAIDs or Helicobacter pylori infection.* <sub>[23.4 ¶fs-id2023452]</sub>

## 40. Temporal bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 7.5; 9.6; 13.4; 14.1; 20.5 · id `temporal` · bone `temporal_bone_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The bone of the lower side of the skull, around the ear (temporal bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1471081; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The temporal bone is the skull bone around your ear. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1471081; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its petrous part houses the middle and inner ear. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It forms the lower side of the skull, below the parietal bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1471081; 7.2 ¶fs-id1975652]</sub>
- It is paired: one on each side of the brain case. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1351960]</sub>
- Its zygomatic process reaches forwards to form the back of the zygomatic arch. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718; 7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is at the side of your head, around and behind your ear. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1471081; 7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>

*Deeper.* Inside the skull, its petrous ridge separates the middle and posterior cranial fossae. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1838522; 7.2 ¶fs-id1489044]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Squamous part.** The flattened upper part. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>
- **Zygomatic process.** Projects forwards to form the back of the zygomatic arch. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>
- **Mastoid process.** A large bump behind the earlobe, where muscles attach. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>
- **Petrous part.** A dense diagonal ridge inside the skull, holding the middle and inner ear. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>
- **External and internal acoustic meatuses.** The ear canal outside; inside, an opening for nerves of hearing, balance and the face. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965; 7.2 ¶fs-id1724550]</sub>
- **Mandibular fossa and articular tubercle.** A hollow and a ridge in front of the ear canal, for the jaw joint. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>
- **Styloid process.** A pen-like spike pointing down, for small muscles and a ligament to the hyoid. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>
- **Stylomastoid foramen and carotid canal.** Openings for the nerve to the face muscles, and a major artery to the brain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>

*Simpler.* It has a flat upper part, a bump behind the ear and a dense ridge that holds the ear. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its name may come from the temple, where hair often turns grey first, marking the passage of time. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1471081]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- Its dense petrous part encloses the small bones and canals of hearing and balance. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718; 14.1 ¶fs-id2479589]</sub>
- In front of the ear canal, the mandibular fossa and articular tubercle make the skull’s half of the jaw joint. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965; 9.6 ¶fs-id1380884]</sub>
- The zygomatic process joins the zygomatic bone, forming the arch from which a chewing muscle arises. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

*Simpler.* It holds your ear, makes the socket for your jaw and anchors a chewing muscle. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882965; 7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joint* **Temporomandibular joint.** The condyle of the mandible meets its mandibular fossa and articular tubercle. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965; 9.6 ¶fs-id1380884]</sub>
- *bone* **Zygomatic bone.** Its temporal process joins the zygomatic process to form the zygomatic arch. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>
- *nerve* **Facial nerve.** Controls the muscles of facial expression; it leaves the skull through the stylomastoid foramen. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965; 13.4 ¶fs-id1271098]</sub>
- *nerve* **Vestibulocochlear nerve.** Carries hearing and balance from the inner ear, through the internal acoustic meatus. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1724550; 14.1 ¶fs-id2479589]</sub>
- *artery* **Internal carotid artery.** Passes through its carotid canal to reach the brain. <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id1278587; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>
- *bones* **Auditory ossicles.** The three tiny bones of the middle ear, inside its petrous part. <sub>[14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>

*Simpler.* It holds the ear and the jaw joint, and important nerves and an artery pass through it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882965]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Its flat squamous part, on the side of the brain case, forms from membrane; its base parts form from cartilage. <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1547037; 7.5 ¶fs-id2251103; 7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>
- The mastoid process enlarges during early childhood. <sub>[7.5 ¶fs-id1241553]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- The mastoid process is larger in males. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (7.2) says it is larger in the male (supporting only).]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A blow that fractures the base of the skull (basilar fracture).  
   **Damaged:** The internal carotid artery, in the carotid canal of the temporal bone.  
   **Patient can’t:** Rely on a normal blood supply to the brain on that side.  
   **Why:** The internal carotid is one of the two main arteries supplying the brain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1972896; 20.5 ¶fs-id2643816]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Swelling or injury of the facial nerve as it leaves the stylomastoid foramen (as in Bell’s palsy).  
   **Damaged:** The facial nerve.  
   **Patient can’t:** Close the eye, smile or raise the eyebrow on that side.  
   **Why:** The facial nerve controls the muscles of facial expression. † <sub>[13.4 ¶fs-id1271098; 7.2 ¶fs-id1882965; † Bell’s palsy and nerve injury at the foramen are not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the nerve’s course and role are.]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the temporal bone takes part in the jaw joint.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965; 9.6 ¶fs-id2608267]</sub>

   - The mandibular fossa is a deep oval hollow in front of the ear canal.
   - The articular tubercle is a smooth ridge just in front of it.
   - The condyle of the mandible sits in the fossa when the mouth is closed.
   - As the mouth opens, it glides forwards onto the tubercle.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the external and internal acoustic meatuses.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965; 14.1 ¶fs-id2744243; 7.2 ¶fs-id1724550]</sub>

   - External: the ear canal, a large opening on the side of the skull.
   - Sound travels in it to the eardrum.
   - Internal: an opening inside the skull, on the petrous ridge.
   - It carries the nerves of hearing, balance and the face.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what you can feel just behind your earlobe, and what it is for.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718; 7.5 ¶fs-id1241553]</sub>

   - The mastoid process.
   - A large bump on the mastoid part of the temporal bone.
   - Muscles attach to it.
   - It enlarges during early childhood.

### Check yourself

1. Which nerve leaves the skull through the stylomastoid foramen?  
   A. Optic nerve · **B. Facial nerve** · C. Trigeminal nerve · D. Hypoglossal nerve  
   *The nerve to the muscles of facial expression, the facial nerve, exits here.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1882965; 13.4 ¶fs-id1271098]</sub>

2. Which part of the temporal bone houses the middle and inner ear?  
   **A. Petrous part** · B. Squamous part · C. Zygomatic process · D. Styloid process  
   *Small cavities inside the petrous ridge house the middle and inner ears.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2010718]</sub>

3. Which artery passes through the carotid canal?  
   A. External carotid artery · B. Vertebral artery · C. Middle meningeal artery · **D. Internal carotid artery**  
   *The internal carotid artery continues through the carotid canal to the brain.* <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id1278587]</sub>

## 41. Thyroid gland

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 17.4 The Thyroid Gland · id `thyroid-gland` · bone `undefined` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A butterfly-shaped gland in front of the trachea that makes the hormones that set the metabolic rate. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750; 17.4 ¶fs-id1241366]</sub>**

*Simpler.* Your thyroid is a small gland in your neck. Its hormones control how fast your body uses energy. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750; 17.4 ¶fs-id1241366]</sub>

*Deeper.* It makes T3 and T4 from iodine, and calcitonin from its parafollicular cells. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1217036; 17.4 ¶fs-id555341]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It lies in front of the trachea, just below the larynx. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>
- The parathyroid glands are embedded in the back of its lobes. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits low in the front of your neck, below your voice box. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its two lobes wrap round the sides of the trachea, joined by the isthmus in front. † <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750; † Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the lobes wrap round the sides of the trachea.]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Isthmus.** The narrow middle part that joins the two lobes. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>
- **Lobes.** Wing-shaped right and left lobes either side of the isthmus. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>
- **Follicles.** Hollow spheres of follicle cells filled with colloid, where the hormones are made. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750; 17.4 ¶fs-id1217036]</sub>
- **Parafollicular (C) cells.** Cells between the follicles that make calcitonin. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id555341]</sub>

*Simpler.* Two wings joined in the middle, full of tiny hormone-making sacs. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>

*Deeper.* Iodine attaches to thyroglobulin in the colloid to build the hormones. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1217036]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- T3 and T4 raise the basal metabolic rate, the energy the body uses at rest. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1241366]</sub>
- They are needed for growth, and especially for the developing nervous system. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1243032]</sub>
- Thyroid hormone release is controlled by thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) from the pituitary, by negative feedback. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1073276]</sub>

*Simpler.* Its hormones turn up the body’s energy use and help children grow. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1241366; 17.4 ¶fs-id1243032]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *airway* **Trachea.** Lies directly behind the gland. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>
- *airway* **Larynx.** Lies just above it. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>
- *gland* **Parathyroid glands.** Embedded in the back of its lobes. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>
- *gland* **Anterior pituitary.** Releases TSH, which stimulates the thyroid. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1073276]</sub>
- *brain* **Hypothalamus.** Releases thyrotropin-releasing hormone (TRH), which triggers TSH secretion. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1073276]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits on the windpipe, below the voice box, and is controlled by the pituitary. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750; 17.4 ¶fs-id1073276]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Its hormones are critical for normal development of the nervous system before birth and in early childhood. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1243032]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- Where diets lack iodine, iodised salt is the main source in many countries. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id768548]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Too little iodine in the diet.  
   **Damaged:** Hormone production in the thyroid follicles.  
   **Patient can’t:** Make T3 and T4: the gland swells into a goitre.  
   **Why:** TSH rises, and colloid builds up in the overstimulated follicles. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1081468]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Inflammation of the thyroid gland.  
   **Damaged:** The thyroid’s hormone output (hypothyroidism).  
   **Patient can’t:** Keep a normal metabolic rate: they gain weight and feel cold.  
   **Why:** Low T3 and T4 slow the use of energy throughout the body. <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1374729; 17.4 ¶fs-id1241366]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why iodine deficiency causes a goitre.** <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1081468; 17.4 ¶fs-id1073276]</sub>

   - Without iodine, T3 and T4 cannot be made.
   - Low T3 and T4 remove negative feedback.
   - TSH secretion increases.
   - Overstimulated follicles store more colloid, and the gland enlarges.

2. *Compare.* **Compare follicle cells and parafollicular cells.** <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750; 17.4 ¶fs-id1217036; 17.4 ¶fs-id555341]</sub>

   - Follicle cells surround the colloid and make T3 and T4.
   - Their hormones raise the metabolic rate.
   - Parafollicular cells lie between follicles and make calcitonin.
   - Calcitonin tends to lower blood calcium.

3. *Predict.* **Predict the TSH level in someone with low T3 and T4 from thyroid inflammation.** <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1073276; 17.4 ¶fs-id1374729]</sub>

   - Low T3 and T4 reduce negative feedback.
   - The hypothalamus releases more TRH.
   - The pituitary releases more TSH.
   - So TSH is high.

### Check yourself

1. Which cells make calcitonin?  
   A. Follicle cells · **B. Parafollicular cells** · C. Chief cells · D. Beta cells  
   *Parafollicular (C) cells between the follicles make calcitonin.* <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id555341]</sub>

2. Which hormone stimulates the thyroid to release T3 and T4?  
   A. TRH · B. ACTH · **C. TSH** · D. Calcitonin  
   *Thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) from the anterior pituitary stimulates release.* <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1073276]</sub>

3. Where is the thyroid gland?  
   **A. In front of the trachea, below the larynx** · B. Behind the oesophagus · C. On top of the kidneys · D. In the mediastinum  
   *It lies anterior to the trachea, just inferior to the larynx.* <sub>[17.4 ¶fs-id1219750]</sub>

---

## 42. Trapezium

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (and glossary); 8.5; 9.4; 9.5 · id `trapezium` · bone `trapezium_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The wrist bone at the base of the thumb, first in the second row of carpal bones (trapezium). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: trapezium]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The trapezium is the wrist bone at the base of your thumb. <sub>[glossary: trapezium; 9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its saddle joint with the first metacarpal gives humans their opposable thumbs. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It is the first bone of the second (distal) row, counting from the thumb side. <sub>[glossary: trapezium]</sub>
- The scaphoid is above it. The trapezoid is beside it, towards the little finger. <sub>[glossary: trapezium]</sub>
- Below it are the first and second metacarpals. <sub>[glossary: trapezium]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits at the base of your thumb, just above the thumb’s palm bone. <sub>[glossary: trapezium; 9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>

*Deeper.* It is one of the four carpal bones the flexor retinaculum attaches to. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Name.** “Trapezium” means table. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- **Saddle surface.** Its surface for the first metacarpal is saddle-shaped: concave one way and convex the other. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>
- **Surface for the second metacarpal.** A small surface beside it, for the second metacarpal. <sub>[glossary: trapezium]</sub>
- **Surfaces for the scaphoid and trapezoid.** It meets the scaphoid above and the trapezoid beside it. <sub>[glossary: trapezium]</sub>
- **Front surface.** With the scaphoid, it anchors the outer end of the flexor retinaculum. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

*Simpler.* It has a saddle-shaped joint surface for the thumb. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>

*Deeper.* The saddle shape lets the thumb move within the plane of the palm and out in front of it. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- The saddle joint is biaxial: the thumb can move in two planes. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>
- Opposition, touching the thumb to a fingertip, combines flexion and abduction at this joint. <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1354267]</sub>
- Its front anchors the flexor retinaculum, which roofs the carpal tunnel. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

*Simpler.* Its saddle shape lets your thumb swing across to touch your fingertips. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120; 9.5 ¶fs-id1354267]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joint* **First carpometacarpal joint.** A saddle joint with the first metacarpal, at the base of the thumb. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>
- *bone* **Second metacarpal.** Meets a small surface beside the saddle. <sub>[glossary: trapezium]</sub>
- *bone* **Scaphoid.** Meets it above. <sub>[glossary: trapezium]</sub>
- *bone* **Trapezoid.** Its neighbour in the second row. <sub>[glossary: trapezium]</sub>
- *ligament* **Flexor retinaculum.** Attached on the thumb side to the trapezium and scaphoid. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

*Simpler.* It carries the thumb’s palm bone and anchors the roof of the carpal tunnel. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120; 8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification), like all limb bones except the clavicle. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Its single ossification centre appears at about the fifth year. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives the fifth year (as the “greater multangular”) (supporting only).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** An injury to the joint at the base of the thumb.  
   **Damaged:** The first carpometacarpal (saddle) joint.  
   **Patient can’t:** Touch the tip of the thumb to the tip of a finger (opposition).  
   **Why:** Opposition is produced at this joint, by flexion and abduction of the thumb. <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1354267; 9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>

2. **Injury:** Years of pinching and gripping.  
   **Damaged:** The joint between the trapezium and the first metacarpal wears (osteoarthritis).  
   **Patient can’t:** Pinch, open jars or turn keys without pain at the base of the thumb.  
   **Why:** This saddle joint takes the force of every pinch and grip. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the trapezium makes the thumb opposable.** <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120; 8.2 ¶fs-id1436751; 9.5 ¶fs-id1354267]</sub>

   - It forms a saddle joint with the first metacarpal.
   - A saddle joint is biaxial: the thumb moves in two planes.
   - The first metacarpal is separate from the others, so it moves freely.
   - Flexion with abduction at this joint brings the thumb to the fingertips.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the trapezium and the trapezoid.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: trapezium; glossary: trapezoid; 8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

   - Both are in the second row, on the thumb side.
   - The trapezium (“table”) is first; the trapezoid (“resembles a table”) is second.
   - The trapezium meets the first and second metacarpals; the trapezoid meets only the second.
   - Only the trapezium anchors the flexor retinaculum.

3. *Predict.* **Predict which thumb movements are lost if the first carpometacarpal joint is fused.** <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1354267; 9.5 ¶fs-id2227353; 9.5 ¶fs-id1751608]</sub>

   - Opposition: touching the thumb to a fingertip.
   - Flexion and extension of the thumb at this joint.
   - Abduction and adduction of the thumb.
   - The joints further along the thumb still bend.

### Check yourself

1. What type of joint lies between the trapezium and the first metacarpal?  
   A. Hinge · B. Pivot · **C. Saddle** · D. Plane  
   *The first carpometacarpal joint is a saddle joint.* <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>

2. Which bones does the trapezium meet below?  
   **A. First and second metacarpals** · B. Second metacarpal only · C. Third metacarpal · D. Fourth and fifth metacarpals  
   *It meets the first and second metacarpals.* <sub>[glossary: trapezium]</sub>

3. Which thumb movement combines flexion and abduction at the first carpometacarpal joint?  
   A. Reposition · B. Extension · C. Adduction · **D. Opposition**  
   *Opposition brings the tip of the thumb to the tip of a finger.* <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1354267]</sub>

---

## 43. Trapezoid

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (and glossary); 8.5; 9.4 · id `trapezoid` · bone `trapezoid_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A small wrist bone at the base of the index finger, second in the second row of carpal bones (trapezoid). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: trapezoid; 8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The trapezoid is a small wrist bone at the base of your index finger. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid; 8.2 ¶fs-id1242304]</sub>

*Deeper.* It meets only one metacarpal, the second, which is firmly anchored and does not move. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid; 8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It is the second bone of the distal row, counting from the thumb side. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>
- The trapezium is beside it on the thumb side, and the capitate on the other side. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>
- The scaphoid is above it, and the second metacarpal below. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits between the base of the thumb and the middle of the wrist. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>

*Deeper.* Ligaments hold it together with the other bones of its row. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id932152]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Name.** “Trapezoid” means “resembles a table”. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- **Surface for the second metacarpal.** Its lower surface, at the carpometacarpal joint. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid; glossary: carpometacarpal joint]</sub>
- **Surface for the scaphoid.** Its upper surface. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>
- **Side surfaces.** It meets the trapezium on one side and the capitate on the other. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a small block of bone with joints on four sides. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its joints with its neighbours are plane (gliding) joints, which allow small sliding movements. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1482998]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- It supports the second metacarpal, which is firmly anchored and does not move. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid; 8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>
- Its plane joints with the neighbouring carpal bones allow only small sliding movements. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1482998]</sub>
- With the other carpal bones it forms the walls and floor of the carpal tunnel. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

*Simpler.* It gives the index finger’s palm bone a firm base. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid; 8.2 ¶fs-id1436751]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Second metacarpal.** Meets its lower surface at the carpometacarpal joint. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid; glossary: carpometacarpal joint]</sub>
- *bone* **Scaphoid.** Meets it above, at the midcarpal joint. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid; 8.2 ¶fs-id932152]</sub>
- *bone* **Trapezium.** Its neighbour on the thumb side. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>
- *bone* **Capitate.** Its neighbour on the little-finger side. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>
- *joints* **Intercarpal joints.** Plane joints between neighbouring carpal bones. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1482998]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is wedged between the trapezium, the capitate, the scaphoid and the second metacarpal. <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification), like all limb bones except the clavicle. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Its single ossification centre appears at about the eighth year. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives the eighth year (as the “lesser multangular”) (supporting only).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Overuse of the tendons or a wrist injury makes the contents of the carpal tunnel swell.  
   **Damaged:** The nerve in the tunnel, whose walls and floor the carpal bones form.  
   **Patient can’t:** Use the hand without pain, numbness or weakness (carpal tunnel syndrome).  
   **Why:** The bony walls and floor and the tough roof cannot stretch, so swelling presses on the nerve. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A hard bony lump on the back of the hand, at the base of the index and middle fingers.  
   **Damaged:** Extra bone (a carpal boss) where the trapezoid and capitate meet the second and third metacarpals.  
   **Patient can’t:** Bend the wrist back fully without pain over the lump.  
   **Why:** The lump catches the tendons that run over the back of the wrist. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the second metacarpal hardly moves.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1436751; glossary: trapezoid; glossary: trapezium; 8.2 ¶fs-id932152]</sub>

   - The second and third metacarpals are firmly anchored in place.
   - The second metacarpal meets the trapezoid (and the trapezium).
   - The distal carpal bones are held together by ligaments.
   - The fourth and fifth metacarpals, by contrast, move a little.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the joints of the trapezoid with those of the trapezium.** <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1482998; glossary: trapezoid; 8.2 ¶fs-id1436751; 9.4 ¶fs-id1837120]</sub>

   - The trapezoid has plane joints with its neighbours, allowing small sliding movements.
   - It meets only the second metacarpal, which is immobile.
   - The trapezium forms a saddle joint with the first metacarpal.
   - So the thumb moves widely while the base of the index finger stays fixed.

3. *Predict.* **Using the order of the carpal bones, predict where the trapezoid lies.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; 8.2 ¶fs-id2328943; glossary: trapezoid]</sub>

   - The second row runs from the thumb side: trapezium, trapezoid, capitate, hamate.
   - So the trapezoid is second from the thumb side.
   - It lies between the trapezium and the capitate.
   - The scaphoid is above it and the second metacarpal below.

### Check yourself

1. Which metacarpal does the trapezoid meet?  
   A. First · **B. Second** · C. Third · D. Fifth  
   *It meets only the second metacarpal.* <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>

2. What type of joints join the trapezoid to its neighbouring carpal bones?  
   A. Saddle joints · B. Hinge joints · **C. Plane (gliding) joints** · D. Pivot joints  
   *Intercarpal joints are plane joints.* <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1482998]</sub>

3. Which bone lies directly above (proximal to) the trapezoid?  
   A. Lunate · B. Triquetrum · C. Radius · **D. Scaphoid**  
   *The scaphoid lies above it.* <sub>[glossary: trapezoid]</sub>

---

## 44. Triquetrum

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb (and glossary); 8.5; 9.4 · id `triquetrum` · bone `triquetrum_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A three-cornered wrist bone, third in the first row of carpal bones (triquetrum). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: triquetrum]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The triquetrum is a small wrist bone on the little-finger side. <sub>[glossary: triquetrum]</sub>

*Deeper.* At the wrist joint it meets the articular disc over the ulna, not bone. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937; reviewer, 2026-11-25]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It is the third bone of the first row, counting from the thumb side. <sub>[glossary: triquetrum]</sub>
- The lunate is beside it on the thumb side. The hamate is below it. <sub>[glossary: triquetrum]</sub>
- The pisiform sits on its front surface. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

*Simpler.* It sits on the little-finger side of the wrist. <sub>[glossary: triquetrum]</sub>

*Deeper.* It is one of only three carpal bones that take part in the wrist joint. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Three corners.** “Triquetrum” means three-cornered. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- **Facet for the pisiform.** A small surface on its front, where the pisiform sits. <sub>[glossary: triquetrum; 8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- **Surface for the wrist joint.** Its upper surface meets the articular disc over the ulna. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937; reviewer, 2026-11-25]</sub>
- **Surface for the hamate.** Its lower surface meets the hamate at the midcarpal joint. <sub>[glossary: triquetrum; 8.2 ¶fs-id932152]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a small three-cornered bone that carries the pisiform on its front. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

*Deeper.* Ligaments join it to the other bones of its row, so the row works as a unit. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- It meets the fibrocartilage pad that spans the radius and the ulna’s styloid process. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>
- It carries the pisiform, which forms the bump at the base of the hand. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>
- Its joint with the hamate is part of the midcarpal joint, which helps the wrist move. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id932152]</sub>

*Simpler.* It helps the wrist move and holds the pisiform in place. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id932152; 8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joint* **Wrist (radiocarpal) joint.** It meets the articular disc over the ulna. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937; reviewer, 2026-11-25]</sub>
- *disc* **Articular disc.** A pad of fibrocartilage between the lower ends of the radius and ulna. <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1061087; 8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>
- *joint* **Midcarpal joint.** Its lower surface meets the hamate. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id932152; glossary: triquetrum]</sub>
- *bone* **Lunate.** Its neighbour on the thumb side, joined to it by ligaments. <sub>[glossary: triquetrum; 8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>
- *bone* **Pisiform.** Sits on its front surface. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; glossary: pisiform]</sub>

*Simpler.* It joins the lunate, the hamate and the pisiform, and the disc over the ulna. <sub>[glossary: triquetrum; 8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification), like all limb bones except the clavicle. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Its single ossification centre appears at about the third year. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives the third year (supporting only).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A fall onto the outstretched hand.  
   **Damaged:** A small chip of bone breaks off the back of the triquetrum: the second most common carpal fracture.  
   **Patient can’t:** Use the wrist without pain on the back of the little-finger side.  
   **Why:** Ligaments and nearby bones strike or pull on its back surface as the wrist is forced backwards. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A twisting fall on the hand.  
   **Damaged:** The articular disc between the ulna and the triquetrum tears.  
   **Patient can’t:** Turn the forearm or grip without pain on the little-finger side.  
   **Why:** The disc unites the lower ends of the radius and ulna, and the triquetrum presses on it. † <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id1061087; † OpenStax describes the disc; a tear of the disc in a fall is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the triquetrum takes part in the wrist joint without touching the ulna.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937; glossary: radiocarpal joint; reviewer, 2026-11-25]</sub>

   - The wrist joint is formed above by the radius and the fibrocartilage pad.
   - The pad spans the radius and the ulna’s styloid process.
   - The triquetrum meets this pad (the articular disc over the ulna).
   - The ulna itself does not touch any carpal bone.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the triquetrum and the pisiform.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; 8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

   - Both are in the first row, on the little-finger side.
   - The triquetrum is three-cornered and third from the thumb side.
   - The pisiform is pea-shaped and sits on the front of the triquetrum.
   - Only the pisiform anchors the flexor retinaculum.

3. *Predict.* **Press the bump at the base of the palm, below the little finger. Predict what you feel, and what lies beneath.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414; 8.2 ¶fs-id2329218]</sub>

   - The bump is the pisiform.
   - It projects forwards from the front of the triquetrum.
   - The triquetrum lies behind it.
   - The flexor retinaculum attaches to the pisiform.

### Check yourself

1. Which bone sits on the front of the triquetrum?  
   A. Hamate · B. Lunate · **C. Pisiform** · D. Capitate  
   *The small pisiform sits on the front of the triquetrum.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1977414]</sub>

2. What does the triquetrum meet at the wrist joint?  
   **A. The articular disc over the ulna** · B. The radius directly · C. The head of the ulna directly · D. The trapezium  
   *It meets the fibrocartilage pad that spans the radius and the ulna’s styloid process.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937; reviewer, 2026-11-25]</sub>

3. Which bone lies below (distal to) the triquetrum?  
   A. Capitate · B. Trapezoid · C. Scaphoid · **D. Hamate**  
   *Its lower surface meets the hamate.* <sub>[glossary: triquetrum]</sub>

---

## 45. Ulna

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb; 8.5; 9.4; 9.5; 9.6; 20.5 · id `ulna` · bone `ulna_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The medial bone of the forearm, on the little-finger side (ulna). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 8.2 ¶fs-id1415448]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The ulna is the forearm bone on the little-finger side. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 8.2 ¶fs-id2002490]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its hooked upper end grips the humerus and forms the hinge of the elbow. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 9.6 ¶fs-id1355969]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It runs from the elbow to the wrist, parallel to the radius, on the medial side. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518]</sub>
- Its upper end forms the bony point of the elbow. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518]</sub>
- At the wrist it does not touch the carpal bones directly. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

*Simpler.* It runs from the point of your elbow down to the little-finger side of your wrist. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518]</sub>

*Deeper.* A pad of fibrocartilage between the radius and the ulna’s styloid process meets the triquetrum. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Trochlear notch.** A large C-shaped hollow, like a crescent spanner. It wraps around the trochlea of the humerus. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518]</sub>
- **Olecranon.** The bony point of the elbow. It fits into the olecranon fossa of the humerus when the elbow straightens. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 8.2 ¶fs-id2444175]</sub>
- **Coronoid process.** The lip below the notch. It fits into the coronoid fossa when the elbow bends fully. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 8.2 ¶fs-id2444175]</sub>
- **Radial notch.** A small smooth area beside the notch, where the head of the radius turns. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518]</sub>
- **Ulnar tuberosity.** A rough area on the front, just below the coronoid process. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518]</sub>
- **Interosseous border.** A ridge on the outer side of the shaft. The interosseous membrane attaches along it. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1266017]</sub>
- **Head and styloid process.** The small rounded lower end, with a short projection behind it. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1266017]</sub>

*Simpler.* Its big hooked top end wraps around the humerus; its small head is at the wrist. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 8.2 ¶fs-id1266017]</sub>

*Deeper.* The ulna’s head is at its lower end. The radius is the other way round: its head is at the elbow. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1266017; 8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- The C-shaped notch wraps around the pulley-shaped trochlea, so the elbow can only bend and straighten. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 9.4 ¶fs-id2523908]</sub>
- The olecranon and coronoid process fit into hollows of the humerus. So the elbow can straighten and bend fully. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2444175]</sub>
- In pronation, it is the radius that turns; its lower end crosses over the ulna. <sub>[9.5 ¶fs-id1837207]</sub>

*Simpler.* Its hook makes a strong hinge, so your elbow bends and straightens like a door. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 9.4 ¶fs-id2523908]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *joint* **Elbow (humeroulnar) joint.** A uniaxial hinge joint between the trochlea and the trochlear notch. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id1355969; 9.4 ¶fs-id2523908]</sub>
- *joint* **Proximal radioulnar joint.** The radial notch meets the head of the radius. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518]</sub>
- *joint* **Distal radioulnar joint.** Its head meets the ulnar notch of the radius. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>
- *ligament* **Ulnar collateral ligament.** A triangular band from the medial epicondyle to the inner side of the upper ulna. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2430675]</sub>
- *membrane* **Interosseous membrane.** Joins the shafts of the ulna and radius. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1266017]</sub>
- *artery* **Ulnar artery.** Runs down the forearm alongside the ulna to the wrist. <sub>[20.5 ¶fs-id1595346]</sub>
- *nerve* **Ulnar nerve.** Passes behind the medial epicondyle and enters the forearm beside the ulna. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

*Simpler.* It hinges with the humerus and joins the radius at both ends. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id1355969; 8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 8.2 ¶fs-id1411842]</sub>

### 6. Development

- It forms by replacing a cartilage model with bone (endochondral ossification). <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>
- Bone first appears in the shaft by about the twelfth week. Centres in the ends appear later, usually after birth. <sub>[8.5 ¶fs-id1397163]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- With the elbow straight, the forearm angles 5–15 degrees outwards from the arm (the carrying angle). <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1483561]</sub>
- The carrying angle is larger in females, to clear their wider pelvis. <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1483561]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** Repeated forceful straightening of the forearm, as in baseball pitching.  
   **Damaged:** The ulnar collateral ligament on the inner side of the elbow.  
   **Patient can’t:** Throw forcefully; the ligament may need reconstruction (“Tommy John surgery”).  
   **Why:** This ligament stops the elbow moving sideways and over-straightening; its front part resists over-straightening most. <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2430675]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A fall onto the point of the elbow.  
   **Damaged:** The olecranon breaks.  
   **Patient can’t:** Straighten the elbow against resistance.  
   **Why:** The muscle that straightens the elbow (triceps) pulls on the olecranon, drawing the fragment away. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the elbow can only bend and straighten.** <sub>[9.4 ¶fs-id2523908; 8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 9.6 ¶fs-id2430675]</sub>

   - The humeroulnar joint is a hinge joint.
   - The C-shaped trochlear notch wraps around the pulley-shaped trochlea.
   - This allows movement around a single axis.
   - Strong collateral ligaments also prevent side-to-side movement.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the upper and lower ends of the ulna and the radius.** <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518; 8.2 ¶fs-id1266017; 8.2 ¶fs-id1411842; 8.2 ¶fs-id1882937]</sub>

   - Ulna: a large upper end with the trochlear notch, and a small head below.
   - Radius: a small disc-shaped head above, and a broad lower end.
   - The lower end of the radius meets two carpal bones.
   - The lower end of the ulna does not touch the carpal bones directly.

3. *Predict.* **A baseball pitcher has pain on the inner side of the elbow when throwing. Predict the injured structure and its normal job.** <sub>[9.6 ¶fs-id2430675]</sub>

   - The ulnar collateral ligament.
   - It runs from the medial epicondyle to the inner side of the upper ulna.
   - It stops sideways movement and over-straightening of the elbow.
   - Forceful repeated throwing strains it; it may need reconstruction.

### Check yourself

1. Which part of the ulna forms the bony point of the elbow?  
   A. Coronoid process · B. Ulnar tuberosity · **C. Olecranon** · D. Styloid process  
   *The olecranon forms the bony tip of the elbow.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518]</sub>

2. Which bone does the trochlear notch of the ulna meet?  
   **A. Humerus** · B. Radius · C. Scaphoid · D. Lunate  
   *It wraps around the trochlea of the humerus at the elbow.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id2080518]</sub>

3. What is the carrying angle?  
   A. The angle of the radial head · B. The bend of the wrist in a Colles fracture · C. The angle between the hand and the forearm · **D. The outward angle of the forearm from the arm**  
   *With the elbow straight, the forearm angles 5–15 degrees outwards from the arm.* <sub>[8.2 ¶fs-id1483561]</sub>

---

## 46. Vomer

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 6.4 · id `vomer` · bone `vomer` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**A single, triangular bone that forms the lower and back part of the nasal septum (vomer). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The vomer is a bone in the middle of your nose, at the lower back. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>

*Deeper.* Seen from behind, it forms the whole height of the nasal septum. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It lies in the midline, in the lower and back part of the nasal septum. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>
- Above it is the perpendicular plate of the ethmoid; in front is the septal cartilage. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>
- It is one of only two unpaired facial bones; the other is the mandible. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1861377]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is in the middle of your nose, at the back and bottom. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>

*Deeper.* Looking into the front of the nose, you see only a small part of it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Triangular shape.** A flat, triangular plate in the midline. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>
- **Upper edge.** Lies below the perpendicular plate of the ethmoid. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>
- **Front edge.** The septal cartilage fills the gap between it and the perpendicular plate. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>
- **Back edge.** Divides the two back openings of the nasal cavity. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is a flat triangle of bone standing upright in the middle of the nose. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>

*Deeper.* In the dry skull, the septal cartilage is missing, leaving a gap in front of it. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- With the ethmoid and the septal cartilage, it divides the nasal cavity into right and left halves. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1211287; 7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>
- The septal cartilage at its front edge extends into the nose and separates the nostrils. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

*Simpler.* It helps split your nose into two separate airways. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1211287; 7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Ethmoid bone.** Its perpendicular plate forms the upper part of the septum. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>
- *cartilage* **Septal cartilage.** Forms the front of the septum, between the vomer and the perpendicular plate. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>
- *space* **Nasal cavity.** The septum divides it into two halves. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1211287]</sub>
- *bone* **Sphenoid bone.** Its upper border meets the body of the sphenoid. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

*Simpler.* It forms the nasal septum with part of the ethmoid and a plate of cartilage. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification). † <sub>[6.4 ¶fs-id1804592; † OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the vomer needs an approved source.]</sub>
- It forms from two thin plates, which are almost completely united by puberty. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (7.2) says so (supporting only).]</sub>

### 7. Variations

- The nasal septum is often bent to one side (a deviated septum). † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A septum bent to one side, from development or an injury.  
   **Damaged:** The bony and cartilage parts of the septum, including the vomer.  
   **Patient can’t:** Breathe freely through the narrower side.  
   **Why:** The septum divides the nasal cavity, so bending it narrows one side. † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1211287; † A deviated septum is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the septum’s role is.]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A blow to the nose that breaks the septum.  
   **Damaged:** The septal cartilage and the vomer.  
   **Patient can’t:** Breathe evenly through both sides of the nose.  
   **Why:** The broken septum shifts into one side of the nasal cavity. † <sub>[† Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain why the vomer looks so different from the front and from behind.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996; 7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

   - From the front, the perpendicular plate forms the upper septum.
   - Only a small part of the vomer shows below it.
   - From behind, looking into the back openings of the nose, the vomer is seen in full.
   - There it forms the whole height of the septum.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the three parts of the nasal septum.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

   - Perpendicular plate of the ethmoid: the upper part.
   - Vomer: the lower and back parts.
   - Septal cartilage: the front, between the two bones.
   - The cartilage also separates the nostrils, and is absent in the dry skull.

3. *Predict.* **Predict what the nasal septum of a dry skull looks like from the front, and explain why.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

   - The perpendicular plate is seen as the upper septum.
   - A small part of the vomer shows below.
   - There is a gap at the front between them.
   - The septal cartilage that fills it in life is not found in the dry skull.

### Check yourself

1. Which part of the nasal septum does the vomer form?  
   A. The upper part · **B. The lower and back part** · C. The front part · D. None of it  
   *It forms the posterior-inferior part of the nasal septum.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1254996]</sub>

2. What fills the gap between the vomer and the perpendicular plate at the front?  
   **A. Septal cartilage** · B. The inferior nasal concha · C. The palatine bone · D. Air  
   *The septal cartilage fills the gap.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2125356]</sub>

3. Which are the two unpaired facial bones?  
   A. Nasal and lacrimal · B. Maxilla and palatine · C. Zygomatic and vomer · **D. Vomer and mandible**  
   *The unpaired facial bones are the vomer and the mandible.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1861377]</sub>
