## 47. Zygomatic bone

*OpenStax A&P 2e: 7.2 The Skull; 6.4 · id `zygomatic` · bone `zygomatic_bone_left` · draft*

### 1. In one line

**The cheekbone, which forms much of the outer wall of the orbit (zygomatic bone). <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036]</sub>**

*Simpler.* The zygomatic bone is your cheekbone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036]</sub>

*Deeper.* Its temporal process forms the front of the zygomatic arch. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036; 7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

### 2. Where it is

- It forms the cheek, at the outer and lower rim of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036]</sub>
- It forms much of the outer (lateral) wall and outer floor of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- Behind, its temporal process joins the zygomatic process of the temporal bone. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

*Simpler.* It is the bone you feel at the top of your cheek, below and beside your eye. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036]</sub>

*Deeper.* The pterion lies a thumb’s width behind its upward part, about two finger widths above the arch. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

### 3. Key features

- **Orbital part.** Forms much of the lateral wall and the lateral floor of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- **Orbital margin.** The outer and lower rim of the front opening of the orbit. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036]</sub>
- **Temporal process.** A short projection backwards, forming the front of the zygomatic arch. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036]</sub>
- **Upward part.** Rises at the outer edge of the orbit; a landmark for finding the pterion. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

*Simpler.* It has a part that walls in the eye socket and a part that reaches back into the arch. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036]</sub>

*Deeper.* The arch works like a drawbridge: two processes, one from each bone, meet in the middle. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

### 4. Form → function

- The lateral wall of each orbit angles 45 degrees away from the midline, widening side (peripheral) vision. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1883481]</sub>
- The thickened margins of the orbit help protect the eye. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1883481]</sub>
- The arch it helps form is a bridge of bone from which a major chewing muscle arises. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

*Simpler.* It shapes your cheek, guards the side of your eye and anchors a chewing muscle. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id2159036; 7.2 ¶fs-id1883481; 7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

### 5. Relations

- *bone* **Temporal bone.** Its zygomatic process joins the temporal process to complete the arch. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>
- *space* **Orbit.** Forms its lateral wall and lateral floor; the maxilla forms the inner floor. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>
- *muscle* **Chewing muscle.** A major muscle that pulls the mandible up in biting and chewing arises from the arch. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>
- *spaces* **Temporal and infratemporal fossae.** The spaces above and below the arch, filled by chewing muscles. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399296]</sub>

*Simpler.* It joins the temporal bone in the arch and forms the outer wall of the eye socket. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208; 7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>

### 6. Development

- Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification). † <sub>[6.4 ¶fs-id1804592; † OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the zygomatic bone needs an approved source.]</sub>
- The flat bones of the face are the last to ossify from membrane. They reach adult size at the end of the adolescent growth spurt. <sub>[6.4 ¶fs-id1639567]</sub>

### 7. Variations

*None from an approved source yet; the app says so.*

### 8. Clinical reasoning

1. **Injury:** A blow to the side of the head, just behind the upward part of the zygomatic bone.  
   **Damaged:** The pterion, with a major artery to the brain’s coverings just inside it.  
   **Patient can’t:** Be left without close watching: a haematoma can build up over hours.  
   **Why:** The pterion is the weakest part of the skull, and bleeding there presses on the brain. <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497; 7.2 ¶fs-id1364278]</sub>

2. **Injury:** A punch or blow to the cheek.  
   **Damaged:** The zygomatic bone or arch breaks and may be pushed inwards.  
   **Patient can’t:** Open the mouth fully, if the broken arch presses on the jaw beneath.  
   **Why:** Part of the mandible and chewing muscles lie just deep to the arch. † <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1399296; † The fracture and its effect on opening the mouth are not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).]</sub>

### 9. Tutorial questions

1. *Explain.* **Explain how the zygomatic arch is built.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

   - It spans from the cheek to just above the ear canal.
   - At the front is the short temporal process of the zygomatic bone.
   - Behind is the longer zygomatic process of the temporal bone.
   - The two join like the ends of a drawbridge.

2. *Compare.* **Compare the parts the zygomatic bone and the maxilla play in the orbit.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927; 7.2 ¶fs-id2159036; 7.2 ¶fs-id1883481]</sub>

   - The zygomatic bone forms the lateral wall and lateral floor.
   - The maxilla forms most of the medial floor.
   - The zygomatic bone also forms the outer, lower rim.
   - The lateral wall angles outwards, widening side vision.

3. *Predict.* **Predict how to find the pterion on a living head, using the zygomatic bone.** <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1248497]</sub>

   - Find the upward part of the zygomatic bone, at the outer edge of the eye.
   - Move a thumb’s width behind it.
   - Then about two finger widths above the zygomatic arch.
   - This is the pterion, the weakest part of the skull.

### Check yourself

1. Which bone forms the front of the zygomatic arch?  
   A. Temporal bone · **B. Zygomatic bone** · C. Maxilla · D. Sphenoid bone  
   *The temporal process of the zygomatic bone forms the front of the arch.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

2. Which part of the orbit does the zygomatic bone mainly form?  
   **A. Lateral wall** · B. Roof · C. Medial wall · D. Back  
   *It forms the lateral wall and lateral floor.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1887927]</sub>

3. What arises from the zygomatic arch?  
   A. A muscle of facial expression · B. The optic nerve · C. The facial nerve · **D. A muscle that pulls the jaw up in chewing**  
   *One of the major muscles that raises the mandible arises from the arch.* <sub>[7.2 ¶fs-id1285208]</sub>

- **Liver.** “Bilirubin is a waste of old red cells that the liver normally excretes in bile.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Jaundice and its causes.
- **Lunate: Median nerve.** “Runs through the carpal tunnel, in front of the lunate.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Lunate.** “Its single ossification centre appears at about the fifth year.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives the fifth year (supporting only).
- **Lunate.** “The dislocated bone takes up space in the tunnel and presses on the median nerve.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Lunate.** “Predict the effect of the lunate dislocating forwards into the carpal tunnel.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Lungs.** “Three lobes on the right, two on the left, branching down to millions of tiny air sacs.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The number of alveoli (“millions”).
- **Lungs.** “Predict what happens to a baby born at 26 weeks without treatment.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The effort of breathing without surfactant (respiratory distress in premature babies).
- **Maxilla.** “Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification).” — OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the maxilla needs an approved source.
- **Maxilla.** “An eye muscle and orbital fat can be trapped in the break.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Metacarpal bones.** “Each has two ossification centres: one for the shaft, and one for the head (in the first, for the base).” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives two centres for each metacarpal (supporting only).
- **Metacarpal bones.** “The first metacarpal ossifies like a phalanx, so some anatomists count the thumb as having three phalanges.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives two centres for each metacarpal (supporting only).
- **Metacarpal bones.** “The fifth metacarpal is the most mobile of the palm bones and takes the force of a badly aimed punch.” — The fracture is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the mobility of the fifth metacarpal is.
- **Metacarpal bones.** “The break enters the saddle joint on which opposition depends.” — The fracture is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the saddle joint and opposition are.
- **Metacarpal bones.** “A man punches a wall and the knuckle of his little finger sinks. Predict the injury and explain it.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Nasal bone: Upper and side edges.** “Meet the frontal bone above and the maxillae at the sides.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Nasal bone: Frontal bone and maxillae.** “Meet them above and at the sides.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Nasal bone.** “Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification).” — OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the nasal bone needs an approved source.
- **Nasal bone.** “The thin bones form the bony bridge, so a blow drives them sideways or inwards.” — OpenStax says these bones break in a broken nose; displacement of the bridge is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Nasal bone.** “The cartilage depends on that lining for nourishment, so trapped blood threatens it.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Nasal bone.** “Explain why a broken nose is usually a break of the nasal bones.” — That cartilage bends rather than breaks is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Occipital bone.** “Its upper part, behind the brain, forms from membrane; its base forms from a cartilage model.” — OpenStax says the top and sides of the brain case form from membrane, and its floor from cartilage; how this divides the occipital bone needs an approved source.
- **Occipital bone.** “The pons and medulla, at the foramen magnum, regulate breathing and the heart.” — Herniation through the foramen magnum is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the brainstem’s role is.
- **Auditory ossicles: Footplate of the stapes.** “Its flat base fills the oval window.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Auditory ossicles.** “The stapes is the smallest bone in the body.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Auditory ossicles.** “They form from cartilage of the embryonic pharyngeal arches.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Auditory ossicles.** “The stapes must move to send vibrations through the oval window.” — Otosclerosis is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the role of the oval window is.
- **Auditory ossicles.** “The malleus is attached to the eardrum, which must vibrate to move the ossicles.” — A torn eardrum is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the eardrum’s role is.
- **Auditory ossicles.** “Predict the effect on hearing if the stapes cannot move, and explain it.” — Hearing loss from a fixed stapes is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the role of the oval window is.
- **Palatine bone.** “Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification).” — OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the palatine bone needs an approved source.
- **Pancreas.** “It develops from buds of the embryonic gut tube that later fuse.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The development of the pancreas.
- **Pancreas.** “Active enzymes digest the pancreatic tissue.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The symptoms of pancreatitis.
- **Parietal bone: Grooves on the inner surface.** “Branches of the artery that supplies the brain’s coverings mark the inner surface of the skull.” — OpenStax says the artery’s branches groove the inner surface of the skull; that this includes the parietal bone needs an approved source.
- **Pisiform.** “It lies within the tendon of a forearm muscle (flexor carpi ulnaris), like a kneecap within its tendon.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Pisiform: Flexor carpi ulnaris.** “Its tendon attaches to the pisiform.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Pisiform: Ulnar nerve and artery.** “Pass beside it into the hand, outside the carpal tunnel.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Pisiform.** “Its single ossification centre appears last of the carpal bones, at about the twelfth year.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives about the twelfth year (supporting only).
- **Pisiform.** “A forearm muscle tendon attaches to it and pulls on the broken bone.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Pisiform.** “The nerve passes beside the pisiform and the hook of the hamate into the hand.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Pisiform.** “After a long ride, a cyclist’s little finger feels numb. Predict the nerve affected and where.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Quadriceps femoris: Femoral nerve.** “A major nerve of the lumbar plexus, to the front of the thigh.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the femoral nerve supplies the quadriceps.
- **Radius.** “The annular ligament normally holds the head in place as it turns.” — “Pulled elbow” (radial head subluxation) in young children is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Rotator cuff.** “A memory aid for the four is SITS: supraspinatus, infraspinatus, teres minor, subscapularis.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The memory aid SITS.
- **Small intestine.** “Three parts: duodenum, jejunum and ileum, lined with millions of tiny folds and fingers.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The number of villi (“millions”).
- **Small intestine.** “It develops from the midgut of the embryo.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The development of the small intestine.
- **Small intestine.** “The surface area for absorption falls sharply.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Conditions that flatten the villi and their effects.
- **Sternocleidomastoid.** “Skeletal muscle of the head develops from general mesoderm; trunk muscles come from somites.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Which embryonic tissue the sternocleidomastoid itself comes from.
- **Sternocleidomastoid.** “Its two heads, sternal and clavicular, can be seen and felt separately low in the neck.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the two heads are visible separately.
- **Sternocleidomastoid.** “The short muscle pulls constantly, tilting to its own side and turning to the opposite.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Congenital torticollis.
- **Sternocleidomastoid.** “Both muscles lose their motor supply from cranial nerve XI.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The accessory nerve’s supply to the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius, and the signs of its injury.
- **Sternocleidomastoid.** “Predict the head position in a baby whose right sternocleidomastoid is short.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). Congenital torticollis.
- **Stomach.** “The J-shaped bag between the oesophagus and the duodenum that holds food, churns it and starts digesting protein.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The stomach’s J shape.
- **Stomach.** “It sits in the upper abdomen, under the diaphragm, mostly on the left.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the stomach lies mostly on the left.
- **Stomach.** “It develops as a widening of the foregut, the same tube that also gives rise to the lung bud.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). The development of the stomach from the foregut.
- **Temporal bone.** “The mastoid process is larger in males.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (7.2) says it is larger in the male (supporting only).
- **Temporal bone.** “The facial nerve controls the muscles of facial expression.” — Bell’s palsy and nerve injury at the foramen are not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the nerve’s course and role are.
- **Thyroid gland.** “Its two lobes wrap round the sides of the trachea, joined by the isthmus in front.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–17, 19–23 and 25). That the lobes wrap round the sides of the trachea.
- **Trapezium.** “Its single ossification centre appears at about the fifth year.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives the fifth year (as the “greater multangular”) (supporting only).
- **Trapezium.** “This saddle joint takes the force of every pinch and grip.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Trapezoid.** “Its single ossification centre appears at about the eighth year.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives the eighth year (as the “lesser multangular”) (supporting only).
- **Trapezoid.** “The lump catches the tendons that run over the back of the wrist.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Triquetrum.** “Its single ossification centre appears at about the third year.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (8.2) gives the third year (supporting only).
- **Triquetrum.** “Ligaments and nearby bones strike or pull on its back surface as the wrist is forced backwards.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Triquetrum.** “The disc unites the lower ends of the radius and ulna, and the triquetrum presses on it.” — OpenStax describes the disc; a tear of the disc in a fall is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Ulna: Ulnar nerve.** “Passes behind the medial epicondyle and enters the forearm beside the ulna.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Ulna.** “The muscle that straightens the elbow (triceps) pulls on the olecranon, drawing the fragment away.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Vomer: Sphenoid bone.** “Its upper border meets the body of the sphenoid.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Vomer.** “Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification).” — OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the vomer needs an approved source.
- **Vomer.** “It forms from two thin plates, which are almost completely united by puberty.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20). Gray’s 1918 (7.2) says so (supporting only).
- **Vomer.** “The nasal septum is often bent to one side (a deviated septum).” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Vomer.** “The septum divides the nasal cavity, so bending it narrows one side.” — A deviated septum is not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20); the septum’s role is.
- **Vomer.** “The broken septum shifts into one side of the nasal cavity.” — Not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
- **Zygomatic bone.** “Like the other flat bones of the face, it forms directly from membrane (intramembranous ossification).” — OpenStax 6.4 says the flat bones of the face form from membrane, but 7.5 says the facial bones form from cartilage; which applies to the zygomatic bone needs an approved source.
- **Zygomatic bone.** “Part of the mandible and chewing muscles lie just deep to the arch.” — The fracture and its effect on opening the mouth are not in OpenStax A&P 2e (chapters 6–9, 12–16, 20).
